Indiana — Cannabis & Hemp: Complete Audited Research, Parts 0–12

Last audited: 30 October 2026.

Scope. This is the whole Indiana commission in one file: Run 1 (Parts 0–6) and Run 2 (Parts 7–12), each audited by a separate fact-checking agent (not the original writer) against primary sources only, with every correction applied in place in the rewritten sections below. The federal baseline was used as given and was not re-audited: scheduling is split (final rule 91 FR 22714, effective 28 April 2026 — FDA-approved cannabis products and state-licensed medical marijuana in Schedule III; all other marijuana, including all adult-use, remains Schedule I); IRS 280E guidance is forthcoming and adult-use activity remains fully subject to 280E; hemp under Public Law 119-37 §781 takes legal effect 12 November 2026 but Public Law 119-103 §2019 limits it until 11 December 2026 to provisions excluding cannabinoids that are not naturally produced or are synthesized, with the full total-THC definition and 0.4 mg per container cap applying after 11 December 2026; USDA enforcement of the DEA-registered-laboratory hemp testing requirement is delayed until 31 December 2026; the federal medical-marijuana appropriations rider is in force through 11 December 2026; no federal cannabis banking law has been enacted. Indiana has no state-licensed medical programme, so marijuana remains Schedule I in Indiana and no Indiana operator gains a 280E lift.

How to read this file. Audit A covers Parts 0–6; Audit B covers Parts 7–12. The rewritten Parts 0–6 follow, then the rewritten Parts 7–12. Verify/Unavailable flags remain only on still-unresolved items. For any licence holder listed as an individual, no personal phone number or email is reproduced — name and link to the official source only.

AUDIT A — RUN 1 (PARTS 0–6): SEPARATE FACT-CHECKING PASS

Last audited: 30 October 2026. This audit was done by a separate fact-checking agent, not the original writer, against primary sources only (Indiana Code and bill/enrolled-act pages on iga.in.gov, Indiana Administrative Code / Indiana Register, OISC, ATC, Indiana State Department of Agriculture, Department of Revenue, Indiana State Police, Attorney General official opinions, federal court records including the Seventh Circuit, Indiana Election Division and county clerks, official tribal government sources, official county/city/town/township sites and codes). Where only a secondary source exists, the claim is marked Unverified. News, law-firm blogs, trade press and AI summaries were never used as confirmation. The federal baseline was used as given and was not re-audited.

Verdict counts (sum exactly to the rows audited): 60 Confirmed, 25 Corrected, 2 Removed, 18 Unverified — 105 rows audited in total. 'Corrected' means the rewritten Parts 0–6 below carry the corrected wording. 'Removed' means the claim was wrong as a programme/rule statement and is deleted from the rewritten text. 'Unverified' means the claim could not be confirmed from a primary source opened in this audit and must not be quoted as settled.

Headline Run 1 audit findings. (1) Run 1's core Indiana position is correct: no adult-use or medical programme, no state cannabis licences, low-THC hemp extract only, and licensed hemp cultivation/handling. (2) The single largest correction is in §1.7: Run 1 says no advertising ban was found, yet HB 1390 (2025), Public Law 227-2025, had already enacted IC 24-4-26, effective upon passage, banning advertising by any medium of marijuana or Schedule I products with AG-only enforcement — that ban is now added and the 'no ban' framing deleted. (3) Paraphernalia is corrected to its three tiers, penalties are corrected to two distinct current sections (§13 $2,500 and §20 $10,000), and 856 IAC is removed as a hemp rule. (4) Two Verify flags are resolved with dates: Dickens transfer denied 2 Dec 2025 and S.D. Ind. 1:23-cv-01115 dismissed 18 Mar 2025 (Doc. 152, jurisdiction/redressability, without prejudice) — each with a verification note against the clerk/docket list. (5) In Parts 5–6, the 2020 press figure is removed from the series, 2024 purpose acreage is added, the 2025 season attribution is corrected to Purdue Extension, the Whitney summary is re-dated to 20 Oct 2023 with its 36-response caveat, and historical Indiana hemp events are listed instead of omitted.

18§1.2Table row '856 IAC / OISC hemp rules' as a hemp rule.Removed856 IAC is the Indiana Board of Pharmacy's controlled-substance/pharmacy rules (IC 35-48-3-1, IC 25-26).Removed as a hemp rule. 20§1.3Possession IC 35-48-4-11: Class B base with no amount threshold; Class A with prior drug conviction OR packaged to appear as low-THC extract; Level 6 only with prior AND ≥30 g / ≥5 g; cultivation charged as possession under (a)(2).ConfirmedIC 35-48-4-11 (mirror); established Parts 7-12 audit.No change.
21§1.3Dealing IC 35-48-4-10 Level 5 triggers as listed (prior dealing + mid-range, or ≥10 lb / ≥300 g, or sale to a minor).CorrectedIC 35-48-4-10(d) (FindLaw text).Corrected: subsection (d)(3) is added — a retailer who sells marijuana, hash oil, hashish or salvia packaged to appear as low-THC hemp extract, knowing or reasonably should have known it was marijuana etc., is also a Level 5 felony. The (b) possession-with-intent evidence rule (evidence beyond weight alone, unless ≥10 lb / ≥300 g) is noted as a gap, not an error.
22§1.3Paraphernalia possession IC 35-48-4-8.3: 'Class C misdemeanour; second offence Class A'.CorrectedIC 35-48-4-8.3 (FindLaw current as of 1 Jan 2026).Corrected: Class C misdemeanour, rising to Class A if the person has a prior unrelated judgment or conviction under the section — 'prior unrelated', not merely 'second offence'. Subsection (a) now also excludes drug-detection items including field test kits and test strips (P.L.53-2025, eff. 1 July 2025). Sentence ranges (Class C up to 60 days / $500; Class A up to 1 year / $5,000) are correct.
23§1.3Dealing in paraphernalia IC 35-48-4-8.5: 'Class A misdemeanour; second offence Level 6 felony' (first tier omitted).CorrectedIC 35-48-4-8.5; established Parts 7-12 audit.Corrected to all three tiers: (a) itself is a Class A infraction with no culpability element; (b) a knowing/intentional violation is a Class A misdemeanour, rising to Level 6 with a prior unrelated judgment or conviction under the section.
24§1.3Conditional discharge IC 35-48-4-12; sentence ranges table (Class B 180 days/$1,000; Class A 1 year/$5,000; Class C 60 days/$500; Level 6 6 months-2.5 years, advisory 1 year/$10,000).ConfirmedFindLaw text; Indiana Court of Appeals opinion quotations.No change. Run 1 does not state a Level 5 range, so nothing to correct there.
25§1.3Public consumption: no standalone public-consumption statute located; possession itself is the offence.ConfirmedSearch-based negative, scoped to Part 3 jurisdictions checked.No change, with Run 1's scope caveat kept.
26§1.3Product types: marijuana flower, edibles, vapes, concentrates, beverages have no lawful Indiana category; only qualifying non-smokable low-THC extract is lawful; smokable hemp excluded and separately criminalised.ConfirmedIC 24-4-21; IC 35-48-4-10.1.No change.
27§1.4Low-THC limit and definition: ≤0.3% total delta-9 including precursors by weight, derived from hemp, no other controlled substances; harvested reproductive organ of the female hemp plant and smokable hemp excluded.ConfirmedIC 24-4-21 (mirror).No change. 35§1.6Prong-(3) framing: the dispute turns on 'contains no other controlled substances' — if a THC variant is itself Schedule I under Indiana law, the product cannot be low-THC extract even at ≤0.3% delta-9.ConfirmedIC 24-4-21 (text analysis, not a court holding).No change, labelled as analysis, not a holding.

37§1.6THCA analysis: THCA counts as a precursor toward the 0.3% total; a non-smokable THCA product within 0.3% total with no other controlled substance would, on the statute's text, be within low-THC extract; smokable THCA flower separately barred.UnverifiedLegal analysis only; no AG section, court ruling or OISC protocol confirming it located.No change. Keep the Verify flag on the current OISC total-THC testing protocol for THCA; do not present the within-limit THCA conclusion as settled law.
38§1.6Dickens, 24A-CR-1912 (Ind. Ct. App. 6 May 2025): Court of Appeals did not decide whether delta-8 is a controlled substance; held only that a charging information alleging Schedule I THC possession was sufficient on its face.CorrectedDickens opinion; Indiana Supreme Court transfer order (copy as searched).Corrected and resolved: the Indiana Supreme Court denied the petition to transfer on 2 Dec 2025 (Rush, C.J., and Goff, J., voted to grant; Molter, J., concurred in denial given the procedural posture). Verify that date against the Appellate Clerk's transfer-disposition list before citing it as clerk-confirmed. Denial of transfer is not a ruling that delta-8 is or is not a controlled substance.
39§1.6Federal AG-opinion challenge, S.D. Ind. No. 1:23-cv-01115: 'dismissed in March 2025 on standing/non-binding-opinion grounds in secondary reports'.CorrectedS.D. Ind. Doc. 152, Order on Cross-Motions for Summary Judgment (govinfo court record, as searched).Corrected: dismissed 18 Mar 2025, Doc. 152, for want of subject-matter jurisdiction in substance because the opinion is not binding and is not law and the plaintiffs showed no redressable injury against the Attorney General alone, without prejudice to re-filing in state court. It is not a ruling that the products are lawful.

41§1.6State response to federal 12 Nov / 11 Dec 2026 dates: no Indiana statute, rule or OISC guidance mirroring PL 119-37 §781 or PL 119-103 §2019 enacted as of 28 Oct 2026; SB 250, which would have mirrored the federal date, died.ConfirmedBill histories; federal baseline as given.No change. 42§1.7Packaging/labelling and infraction penalty; child-resistant packaging as proposed only (SB 478 / SB 250), not current law.ConfirmedIC 24-4-21-3/-4/-5.No change.
43§1.7Advertising: 'No hemp-specific advertising statute was located in current law' and 'no hemp advertising ban ... was found'.CorrectedHB 1390 (2025), Public Law 227-2025, enrolled act HB1390.06.ENRS §41 (iga PDF, directly opened in the Parts 7-12 audit).Corrected (major omission): HB 1390 added IC 24-4-26 (Advertising of Illegal Products), effective UPON PASSAGE (not 1 July 2025), prohibiting advertising by any medium of (1) a product containing marijuana or (2) a Schedule I controlled substance, with contract grandfathering, AG-only enforcement (injunction, up to $5,000 first / $10,000 second / $15,000 each additional violation, plus reasonable costs, penalties to the state general fund), and NOT added as a deceptive consumer sales act. It is not hemp-specific in form, but it directly covers marijuana advertising and, on the AG's reading, Schedule I THC-variant products. Run 1's 'no advertising ban' framing is deleted; SB 250's advertising restrictions died, which remains correct.
44§1.7Delivery / online sales: no current state ban on online or delivery sales of qualifying low-THC extract; SB 250's proposed online/delivery ban died; hemp shipment under IC 15-15-13-11; smokable-hemp shipment only by continuous-transit exception.ConfirmedIC 15-15-13-11; SB 250 history.No change. 60Part 2State sales tax 7%, no general local sales-tax add-on (local taxes that exist are county innkeeper's, food-and-beverage and local income taxes, not a general local sales tax).Confirmedin.gov DOR sales-tax and county-tax pages.No change. 67Part 3Home-rule framing: Run 1 says courts 'have applied that limit to strike local ordinances that impose duties on activities regulated by a state agency or prohibit conduct authorised under a state licence/permit', citing Town of Avon v. West Central Conservancy District 'summarised in secondary planning-law sources'.UnverifiedIC 36-1-3-8(a)(7) confirmed via Justia mirror and Indiana Supreme Court, Duke Energy Indiana LLC v. City of Noblesville, 23S-PL-130 (30 May 2024).No change to the statute, corrected framing in the audit: (a)(7) withholds 'the power to regulate conduct that is regulated by a state agency, except as expressly granted by statute', but Duke Energy holds the utility code does not automatically preempt local ordinances and asks whether the specific state code occupies the field — (a)(7) is a withholding, not an automatic strike-down. AUDIT B — RUN 2 (PARTS 7–12): ALREADY AUDITED, CARRIED OVER

The Parts 7–12 audit below is the audit delivered on 30 October 2026 (75 rows: 43 Confirmed, 6 Corrected, 1 Removed, 25 Unverified, as updated by the Parts 7, 9–10 and 11–12 checker passes). Its verdict counts are separate from Audit A above; the combined commission therefore carries 180 audit rows in total across Parts 0–12.

PART 0 — INDEPENDENT AUDIT (30 October 2026)

Last audited: 30 October 2026. This audit was done by a separate fact-checking pass on the Run 2 report (Parts 7–12, researched 28–29 October 2026). The auditor did not write the original report. Federal law in the brief was taken as given and was not re-audited; only the report's statement of it and Indiana's responses to it were checked. Primary sources were preferred throughout: the Indiana Code and iga.in.gov bill/enrolled-act pages, the Indiana Administrative Code and Indiana Register, OISC (Purdue), the Alcohol and Tobacco Commission, the State Department of Agriculture, the Department of Revenue, the Indiana State Police, Attorney General official opinions, federal court records including the Seventh Circuit, the Indiana Election Division, official tribal government sources, and official county/city/town sites and codes. Where only a secondary source exists, the claim is marked Unverified, not Confirmed. Quotations taken from Justia/FindLaw/LawServer mirrors of the Indiana Code are labelled as mirrors in the audit — the current iga.in.gov text should be rechecked before any filing, licence application or enforcement decision.

Verdict counts (sum exactly to the rows audited): 43 Confirmed, 6 Corrected, 1 Removed, 25 Unverified — 75 rows audited in total. 'Corrected' means the rewrite carries the corrected wording and the old wording appears nowhere in the rewritten report. 'Removed' means the claim was wrong as a programme count and no correct version of it is carried forward. Nothing in the audit table is itself a substitute for the rewritten report that follows it.

Headline audit findings. (1) The report's core Indiana position is correct: Indiana has no adult-use or medical programme, no state cannabis licences, and its own USDA-approved hemp plan (not USDA direct licensing). (2) The genuine corrections that change the text are the HB 1390 enrolled-act number, Code section, effective date and penalty schedule (Public Law 227-2025; new IC 24-4-26, effective upon passage) and the removal of conflicting Plan section numbers (§59 vs §52) for the minimum-size provisions. (3) The federal-baseline application is completed with the verbatim IC 35-48-2-1(e) automatic-follow text, which the original report described but did not quote. (4) Everything the original report already flagged as Verify stays flagged unless this audit could confirm it from a primary source — most notably expungement fees and waiting-period details, neighbour-state figures, polling figures, and plan-level licence details that live only in search-index extracts of the USDA Plan PDF.

42Part 9 §9.2HB 1390 (2025) advertising ban: enacted, effective 1 July 2025, prohibits advertising 'by any medium' of (1) a product containing marijuana or (2) a Schedule I controlled substance; House conference 81–9 (23 Apr 2025), Senate conference 31–19 (24 Apr 2025), House third reading 90–0 (13 Feb 2025).CorrectedEnrolled act HB1390.06.ENRS (iga, 41 pages), Section 41.Corrected from the enrolled act itself: HB 1390 (2025), Public Law 227-2025, adds a new chapter IC 24-4-26 (Advertising of Illegal Products), effective UPON PASSAGE — not 1 July 2025. Enforcement is by the Attorney General only (IC 24-4-26-5): injunction, civil penalties up to $5,000 for a first violation, $10,000 for a second, $15,000 for each additional violation, plus the AG's reasonable investigation and action costs, with penalties deposited in the state general fund. Advertising is NOT added as a deceptive consumer sales act — the Section 42 consumer-transaction amendment concerns emergency towing, not marijuana advertising. REWRITTEN REPORT — PARTS 0–6 (AUDITED)

Federal baseline — applied to Indiana, not re-researched

The user-supplied federal baseline is used as fixed context: (a) state-licensed medical marijuana and FDA-approved products moved to Schedule III effective 28 April 2026 (91 FR 22714); all adult-use marijuana remains Schedule I; the broader 2024 rescheduling proposal is not final. (b) IRS §280E is generally lifted for Schedule III medical activity; adult-use remains fully subject to §280E. (c) Hemp: PL 119-37 §781 takes effect 12 November 2026, but PL 119-103 §2019 limits it until 11 December 2026; after 11 December 2026 the full total-THC hemp definition and the 0.4 mg total THC per container cap apply. (d) USDA enforcement of DEA-registered lab testing for hemp is delayed to 31 December 2026. (e) The federal medical marijuana appropriations rider runs through 11 December 2026. (f) There is no federal cannabis banking law.

Indiana application — the central finding of this run: Indiana has no state-licensed medical marijuana programme. There is no Indiana medical licence, registry, dispensary, or patient card to which the Schedule III medical carve-out could attach. On the facts found in this run, therefore, marijuana remains Schedule I in Indiana under the split scheduling for state-law purposes, and no Indiana operator gains a §280E lift from the federal medical move unless and until Indiana creates a state-licensed medical programme. This is an application of the supplied baseline to the Indiana fact of “no programme,” not a new federal finding. Source for no programme: IC 35-48-4-11 and the absence of any medical programme statute in IC Title 35, Art. 48, discussed in Part 1; secondary confirmation, Dr. Green Relief, reviewed 14 Oct 2026, Indiana medical card page, accessed 28 Oct 2026.

Does Indiana law automatically follow federal rescheduling? Partly, and with a brake. IC 35-48-2-1(e) provides, in substance, that if a substance is rescheduled to a less restrictive schedule or deleted under federal law, “the substance is rescheduled or deleted under this article” — unless the Board (the Indiana Board of Pharmacy, as successor to the former controlled substances advisory committee) objects: it must notify the chairman of the legislative council not more than 30 days after the federal change, and the substance “may not be rescheduled or deleted until the conclusion of the next complete session of the general assembly,” with the notice published. Text via Justia, IC 35-48-2-1, IC 35-48-2-1, accessed 28 Oct 2026. Two limits matter here and are flagged Verify for a definitive legal conclusion: (1) the federal action as supplied is a split scheduling (medical/FDA-approved only), not a wholesale rescheduling of “marijuana” as a substance, so it is not settled that IC 35-48-2-1(e) would move Indiana’s statutory Schedule I listing of marijuana at all; (2) no Board objection, Board report, or legislative-council notice concerning the April 2026 federal action was located in this run. Do not read IC 35-48-2-1(e) as “Indiana automatically legalised medical marijuana in April 2026.” It did not — Indiana still has no medical statute, and possession/dealing remain criminal under IC 35-48-4-10 and -11.

Contradiction check against the baseline: No Indiana statute or official source found in this run contradicts the baseline dates (12 Nov / 11 Dec 2026). What Indiana has failed to do is enact a state mirror: 2026 SB 250 would have mirrored the federal date, and it died (Part 1 §10). The gap between federal law after 11 Dec 2026 and unchanged Indiana state law is therefore the main open question for Run 2 follow-up, not a contradiction of the baseline itself.

Direct-read failure note: The official Indiana General Assembly bill page for 2026 SB 250 (iga.in.gov/legislative/2026/bills/senate/250) returned no extractable text to the text reader on 28 Oct 2026. Its status below is therefore corroborated from Legislative Services Agency fiscal notes (official LSA PDFs) and multiple secondary reports, and is marked Verify for direct iga confirmation. Update 28 Oct 2026: a live check of that exact URL has now returned and confirms the Senate passage and House-committee history below (Senate Roll Call 123, 35–13, 28 Jan 2026; House committee report “amend do pass, adopted” 19 Feb 2026; last listed action 23 Feb 2026, cosponsors added; no House passage or enactment listed). The iga page itself does not carry a “died” label, so the “not enacted” conclusion in §10 rests on the absence of House passage/enactment plus the end of the 2026 short session, as explained there.

Legal statusIllegal — both recreational and medical. No adult-use programme, no medical programme, no patient registry, no cannabis licences. Only the narrow low-THC hemp extract regime and licensed hemp cultivation/handling are lawful.IC 35-48-4-11; IC 15-15-13; see Part 1.
Schedule in IndianaMarijuana and tetrahydrocannabinols are listed in Schedule I, IC 35-48-2-4(d). Because Indiana has no state-licensed medical programme, the federal Schedule III medical carve-out has no Indiana licensee to attach to.IC 35-48-2-4(d); AG Opinion 2023-1; baseline application above.
Hemp extract / CBDLawful only as “low THC hemp extract”: derived from hemp as defined in IC 15-15-13-6, ≤0.3% total delta-9 THC including precursors by weight, and containing no other controlled substances. QR-code / barcode labelling and an independent-lab certificate of analysis are required (IC 24-4-21-3, -4). No state minimum age in current statute; no retail licence or registry for finished extract.IC 35-48-1-17.5 (now recodified — see Part 1 note); IC 24-4-21-3, -4.
Smokable hempBanned as a retail / possession matter: dealing in smokable hemp is a Class A misdemeanor, IC 35-48-4-10.1, with a continuous-transit exception added by SEA 335 (2020). Separately, hemp bud/flower may be sold only to a processor licensed under IC 15-15-13-19. The 2019–2021 federal litigation did not strike the ban down (see Part 1 §5).IC 35-48-4-10.1; IC 15-15-13-19; C.Y. Wholesale v. Holcomb, 965 F.3d 541 (7th Cir. 2020).
Intoxicating hempUnresolved / high-risk grey area. Delta-8, delta-10, THCP, THC-O and similar variants are, in AG Official Opinion 2023-1 (12 Jan 2023), Schedule I controlled substances under IC 35-48-2-4(d)(31). An AG opinion is not statute and does not bind courts. Products are nevertheless widely sold; no comprehensive state retail licensing, age, potency, or testing regime for intoxicating hemp has been enacted.AG Opinion 2023-1, in.gov PDF.

There is still no ATC hemp retailer/manufacturer/distributor/carrier permit in current law.SB 478 (2025) and SB 250 (2026) — Part 1 §10. None passed a chamber in the form needed to become law; none is current law.Part 1 §10, with bill-status sources.

Indiana has enacted no state mirror as of this run. PART 1: LAWS

1.1 Legal status — confirmed from statute

Recreational: illegal. Medical: illegal — no programme exists. Low-THC/CBD only: yes, within the low-THC hemp extract limits. Otherwise: illegal. The criminal code makes it an offence to possess, grow, deal in, or manufacture marijuana, hash oil, hashish, or salvia, subject only to the statutory exclusions for hemp, low-THC hemp extract, and smokable hemp (the last exclusion is from the marijuana definition; smokable hemp is separately criminalised — see §1.5). There is no “medical necessity,” out-of-state card, or physician-certification defence in the current Indiana Code.

Recodification warning (2025). A June 2026 Indiana Court of Appeals opinion notes that IC chapter 35-48-1 was repealed effective 1 July 2025 and the marijuana definition is now codified at IC 35-48-1.1-29, still excluding hemp as defined by IC 15-15-13-6. Wemes v. State, Opinion 25A-CR-2491 (Ind. Ct. App. 3 June 2026), public.courts.in.gov, accessed 28 Oct 2026. Older sources therefore cite IC 35-48-1-19, IC 35-48-1-17.5, and IC 35-48-1-26.6 for the same concepts. This file gives both numbers where the shift matters and marks the exact current numbering Verify against the current official iga Code text before any filing or advice use. The substance of the definitions below was confirmed in the pre-2025 text and in the 2026 court discussion; no substantive change to the 0.3% threshold was located.

1.2 Every state statute and regulation — table

CitationSubject / plain-English summaryOperative text (verbatim, abridged only by “…”)Last amended / enactedOfficial / source link
IC 15-15-13-6“Hemp” definition“‘Hemp’ means the plant Cannabis sativa L. and any part of that plant, including the seeds thereof and all derivatives, extracts, cannabinoids, isomers, acids, salts, and salts of isomers, whether growing or not, with a delta-9-tetrahydrocannabinol concentration of not more than three-tenths of one percent (0.3%) on a dry weight basis, for any part of the Cannabis sativa L. plant.”Added P.L.165-2014; amended P.L.190-2019 (SEA 516), §6OISC statute PDF, IC 15-15-13 (2021 text); USDA Indiana Hemp Plan, ams.usda.gov PDF. Current-text recheck Verify.
IC 15-15-13-7Hemp licensing; grower/handler licences; application contents“(a) Subject to section 15 of this chapter, the production of, possession of, scientific study of, and commerce in hemp … is authorized in Indiana. Hemp is subject to regulation by the state seed commissioner… (b) All growers and handlers must have a hemp license issued by the state seed commissioner. Growers and handlers engaged in the production of agricultural hemp seed must also have an agricultural hemp seed production license.” Application must include name/address of applicant and operation, GPS coordinates and legal description, acreage (growers), a perjury statement of no drug-related felony/misdemeanor conviction in the previous 10 years, consent to a state/national criminal-history background check, consent to aerial/physical inspection, a nonrefundable application fee (amount set by the seed commissioner, including background-check cost), and any other information required.Added P.L.165-2014; amended P.L.190-2019, §8LawServer IC 15-15-13-7 (secondary mirror of statute text); OISC PDF above. IC 15-15-13-6.5“Hemp product”A product derived from or made from hemp, excluding (1) smokable hemp as defined in the criminal code and (2) products over 0.3% total delta-9 by weight. This exclusion is why a finished CBD oil can be a “hemp product” while hemp bud/flower for smoking is not.Added P.L.190-2019OISC statute PDF; secondary summary LegalClarity smokable hemp guide, accessed 28 Oct 2026.
IC 15-15-13-9, -11, -12, -18, -20, -21Seed, transport, testing, receipts, penalties, local preemptionSee §§1.7–1.8 and Part 3. Key texts: §19 — “Hemp bud … and hemp flower … may be sold only to a processor licensed under this chapter.” §21 — “A county, city, town, or township may not adopt or enforce an ordinance restricting or regulating: (1) the growth, production, or processing of hemp; or (2) any subject regulated by this chapter.” §20(b) — knowingly/intentionally growing, handling, or selling agricultural hemp seed without a licence (excluding smokable hemp) is a Class A misdemeanor; §20(a) civil penalty up to $10,000 per violation for knowing/intentional licence/rule violations, plus possible revocation (a separate, also-current $2,500 cap applies under IC 15-15-13-13(b) — the two caps belong to different sections, §13 and §20, and neither is a draft proposal).P.L.190-2019, §§19–22OISC statute PDF, IC 15-15-13 PDF.
IC 35-48-1-19 / now 35-48-1.1-29“Marijuana” definition and exclusions“‘Marijuana’ means any part of the plant genus Cannabis whether growing or not; the seeds thereof; the resin extracted from any part of the plant, including hashish and hash oil; any compound, manufacture, salt, derivative, mixture, or preparation of the plant, its seeds or resin.” The term does not include: mature stalks, fiber from stalks, oil/cake from seeds, other stalk preparations (except resin), sterilized seed incapable of germination, hemp (as defined by IC 15-15-13-6), low-THC hemp extract, or smokable hemp.Amended P.L.153-2018 (SEA 52), §22; P.L.190-2019, §29; recodified eff. 1 July 2025P.L.190-2019 text, P.L.190-2019 PDF; Wemes (2026) for recodification.
IC 35-48-1-17.5 / now 35-48-1.1-27 (numbering Verify)“Low THC hemp extract”“‘Low THC hemp extract’ means a substance or compound that: (1) is derived from or contains any part of the plant Cannabis sativa L. that meets the definition of hemp under IC 15-15-13-6; (2) contains not more than three-tenths percent (0.3%) total delta-9-tetrahydrocannabinol (THC), including precursors, by weight; and (3) contains no other controlled substances.” It does not include: (1) the harvested reproductive organ, whether immature or mature, of the female hemp plant; or (2) smokable hemp.Added P.L.153-2018 (SEA 52), §21; amended P.L.190-2019, §28LawServer IC 35-48-1-17.5; court discussion in Laramore v. State, 22A-CR-716 (Ind. Ct. App. 1 Nov 2022).
IC 35-48-1-17.2, -17.3, -26.6Hemp bud, hemp flower, smokable hemp“Hemp bud” = the harvested immature reproductive organ of the female hemp plant (excluding agricultural hemp seed). “Hemp flower” = the harvested reproductive organ, whether immature or mature, of the female hemp plant (excluding agricultural hemp seed). “Smokable hemp” (pre-2025 numbering §26.6): “a product containing not more than three-tenths percent (0.3%) delta-9-tetrahydrocannabinol (THC), including precursors and derivatives of THC, in a form that allows THC to be introduced into the human body by inhalation of smoke,” including hemp bud and hemp flower; excluding a hemp plant or parts grown/handled by a licensee for processing/manufacturing into a legal hemp product.Added P.L.190-2019, §§26–30P.L.190-2019 PDF above.
IC 35-48-2-4(d)Schedule I — marijuana and tetrahydrocannabinolsSchedule I includes marijuana and “Tetrahydrocannabinols, including synthetic equivalents of the substances contained in the plant, or in the resinous extractives of Cannabis, sp. and synthetic substances, derivatives and their isomers with similar chemical structure and pharmacological activity such as: (A) 1 cis or trans tetrahydrocannabinol, and their optical isomers; (B) 6 cis or trans tetrahydrocannabinol, and their optical isomers; and (C) 3,4 cis or trans tetrahydrocannabinol and their optical isomers,” with compounds of these structures covered regardless of numerical designation of atomic positions. Subsection numbering for this paragraph is cited as (d)(31) in AG Opinion 2023-1 and as (d)(32) in the Dickens transfer petition — numbering conflict, Verify against current Code.Original Acts 1976, P.L.148; later amendments (see Part 1 note)AG Opinion 2023-1 PDF; Dickens petition excerpts via search results, accessed 28 Oct 2026.
IC 35-48-2-1(e)Federal rescheduling follow-through (with Board objection brake)“If a substance is rescheduled to a less restrictive schedule or deleted as a controlled substance under federal law, the substance is rescheduled or deleted under this article. If the board objects to inclusion, rescheduling, or deletion of the substance, the board shall notify the chairman of the legislative council not more than thirty (30) days after the federal law is changed and the substance may not be rescheduled or deleted until the conclusion of the next complete session of the general assembly. The notice … must be published.” For a more restrictive federal move, the board “shall recommend similar control” unless it objects after a hearing.Amended most recently P.L.84-2010, §92 (per Justia history line)Justia IC 35-48-2-1, accessed 28 Oct 2026.

IC 35-48-4-7; IC 35-48-4-2Possession / dealing in Schedule I/II controlled substances generallyThese are the general controlled-substance offences that prosecutors use for THC-variant cases pleaded as Schedule I THC (see Dickens, §1.6). Marijuana-specific offences in §§10–11 are the usual charge for plant marijuana; THC concentrates pleaded as a Schedule I controlled substance can be charged under §§2/7 instead. Exact charging practice is prosecutor-dependent — Verify for any individual case.—State v. Dickens, 24A-CR-1912 (Ind. Ct. App. 6 May 2025), Justia.
USDA-approved Indiana Hemp Plan (22 Oct 2020) [856 IAC removed — see audit: 856 IAC is the Board of Pharmacy's rules, not an OISC hemp rule]Hemp programme rules and planIndiana operates under a USDA-approved state plan (USDA approval press release 22 Oct 2020, per OISC archived page), not the USDA direct programme. The plan document is the USDA PDF linked in the table above. Individual rule citations (e.g. emergency rule LSA #23-531) were listed on the archived OISC page but their current Indiana Register status was not re-verified in this run — Verify.Plan approved Oct 2020OISC archived hemp page, oisc.purdue.edu/hemp/other_info.html, accessed 28 Oct 2026.

What was not found as current law: no Indiana medical cannabis act, no adult-use act, no hemp-retail permit act, no hemp excise act, no state seed-to-sale tracking act for finished products, and no employment-protection act for cannabis use. The 2017 epilepsy-only CBD registry (HEA 1148, 2017) was superseded/repealed in substance by SEA 52 (2018), which opened low-THC hemp extract to general distribution subject to labelling/testing; no registry survives in IC 24-4-21 as found in this run.

1.3 Possession, cultivation, dealing, paraphernalia, public consumption, product types

ConductStatuteClassificationSentence / fine (general Indiana sentencing ranges)
Possession of marijuana, hash oil, hashish, or salvia — any amount, no prior drug conviction; also knowingly/intentionally growing/cultivating marijuana, or knowingly failing to destroy marijuana growing on one’s premisesIC 35-48-4-11(a)Class B misdemeanorUp to 180 days; fine up to $1,000 (IC 35-50-3-3)

Conditional discharge (one-time, misdemeanor possession only)IC 35-48-4-12Not a separate penalty — procedureIf a person with no prior controlled-substance conviction pleads guilty to misdemeanor possession of marijuana, hashish, salvia, or smokable hemp, the court, without entering judgment and with the person’s consent, may defer proceedings under court conditions; on fulfilment the court shall dismiss the charges; on violation it may enter judgment. Only one dismissal per person under this section. Text (FindLaw, current as of 1 Jan 2026): “If a person who has no prior conviction … pleads guilty to possession of marijuana, hashish, salvia, or smokable hemp as a misdemeanor, the court, without entering a judgment of conviction and with the consent of the person, may defer further proceedings…”

Cultivation: There is no separate “cultivation” penalty table for marijuana in Indiana. Growing/cultivating marijuana is charged as possession under IC 35-48-4-11(a)(2), and manufacturing as dealing under IC 35-48-4-10. Unlicensed growing/handling of hemp is a different matter: knowing/intentional unlicensed growing, handling, or agricultural hemp seed sale (excluding smokable hemp) is a Class A misdemeanor under IC 15-15-13-20(b), plus civil penalties.

Public consumption: No standalone “public consumption of marijuana” statute was located. Public use is reached through possession itself (any possession is an offence), plus general public-intoxication and disorderly provisions where they apply, and — for hemp — the smokable-hemp ban. Do not present a separate public-consumption fine table as Indiana law; none was found. Verify for any local public-consumption ordinance (none located for the Part 3 jurisdictions).

Product types — marijuana (all illegal): Flower, edibles, vapes, concentrates, and beverages made from marijuana (over 0.3% delta-9 THC, or otherwise not qualifying as low-THC hemp extract) are all within the marijuana / controlled-substance offences above; Indiana law does not create a lawful edible, vape, concentrate, or beverage category for marijuana. Product types — hemp (lawful only if they qualify): Non-smokable low-THC hemp extract products (oils, tinctures, edibles, topicals, beverages that meet the 0.3% total delta-9 + no-other-controlled-substance test and the labelling/testing rules) are the lawful category. Smokable hemp (bud, flower, pre-rolls, and any product in a form that allows THC to enter by inhalation of smoke) is excluded from low-THC hemp extract and separately criminalised. Vapes that deliver a THC variant other than trace delta-9 fall into the §1.6 grey area, not into a clearly lawful category.

1.4 Low-THC hemp extract — THC limit, labelling, QR code, testing, who may sell, age, registry, out-of-state cards

THC limit: Not more than 0.3% total delta-9 THC, including precursors, by weight (IC 35-48-1-17.5(a)(2)). Note the word “total” and “including precursors”: THCA, as a precursor, counts toward that total in the definition as written. The hemp-plant definition in IC 15-15-13-6 is phrased differently — delta-9 THC concentration not more than 0.3% on a dry weight basis for any part of the plant — which is why finished-product and crop compliance are not identical calculations. Verify the current official calculation method (post-decarboxylation / total THC) in the current OISC rule, which was not re-verified in this run.

IC 24-4-21-2 (distribution gateway). “A person may distribute low THC hemp extract in Indiana only if the product: (1) has been approved by the federal Food and Drug Administration or the federal Drug Enforcement Agency as a prescription or over the counter drug; or (2) meets the requirements of this chapter.” Via 2026 SB 250 draft amending IC 24-4-21-2 (which quotes the then-current text), accessed 28 Oct 2026.

IC 24-4-21-3 (certificate of analysis). “A person may distribute low THC hemp extract in Indiana only if the distributor has a certificate of analysis prepared by an independent testing laboratory showing: (1) that the low THC hemp extract is the product of a batch tested by the independent testing laboratory; (2) that the independent testing laboratory determined that the batch contained not more than three-tenths percent (0.3%) total delta-9-tetrahydrocannabinol (THC), including precursors, by weight, based on the testing of a random sample of the batch; and (3) the cannabidiol percent present of the low THC hemp extract.” Later drafts add “by weight or volume” — that addition is in proposed 2026 text, not presented here as current law.

IC 24-4-21-4(a) (packaging). “Except as provided in subsection (b), low THC hemp extract must be distributed in packaging that contains the following information: (1) A scannable bar code or QR code linked to a document that contains information with respect to the manufacture of the low THC hemp extract, including the: (A) batch identification number; (B) product name; (C) batch date; (D) expiration date, which must be not more than two (2) years from the date of manufacture; (E) batch size; (F) total quantity produced; (G) ingredients used, including the: (i) ingredient name; (ii) name of the company that manufactured the ingredient; (iii) company or product identification number or code, if applicable; and (iv) ingredient lot number; and (H) download link for a certificate of analysis for the low THC hemp extract. (2) The batch number. (3) The Internet address of a web site to obtain batch information. (4) The expiration date. (5) The number of milligrams of low THC hemp extract. (6) The manufacturer. (7) The fact that the product contains not more than three-tenths percent (0.3%) total delta-9-tetrahydrocannabinol (THC), including precursors, by weight.” Subsection (b) in the original text allowed distribution without meeting these requirements before 1 July 2018; that grace period is long expired.

IC 24-4-21-5 (penalty). “A person who distributes low THC hemp extract in violation of this chapter commits a Class B infraction. However, the offense is a Class A infraction if the person has a prior unrelated judgment for a violation of this chapter.” These penalties are in addition to any criminal penalties for unlawful possession/distribution of a controlled substance.

Who may sell: Current law imposes the distributor-side duties above (FDA/DEA approval or chapter compliance, COA, packaging) but, as found in this run, does not create a state retail licence, permit, or registration for finished low-THC hemp extract sellers. Any retailer is still subject to general business requirements (business registration, Registered Retail Merchant Certificate for sales tax — Part 2) and, if it also sells tobacco/vaping products, the ATC tobacco regime. That absence is the gap the failed SB 478 (2025) and SB 250 (2026) bills tried to fill.

Minimum age: None in current IC 24-4-21 as found. No statewide 18+ or 21+ purchase age for low-THC hemp extract or CBD was located. Proposed 21+ rules (SB 478, SB 250, HB 1275 in 2026) all died or remained in committee. Voluntary retailer 21+ policies are not law. Tobacco/vaping age 21 (IC 35-46-1-10) applies to tobacco/e-cigarettes, not to hemp extract as such.

Registry / medical use provision: None current. The 2017 epilepsy-only CBD registry was not carried forward as a registry in SEA 52 (2018). There is no Indiana patient registry, physician certification, or medical-use card for low-THC hemp extract in current law.

Out-of-state medical cards: Not recognised. No statute recognising another state’s medical marijuana card, reciprocity, or visiting-patient provision was located. Possession of marijuana in Indiana remains an offence even if the person holds another state’s card. This is stated as a negative finding from the absence of any reciprocity statute in IC Title 35, Art. 48 and IC 24-4-21 as searched in this run — Verify before advising any individual, but no contrary statute was found.

Labelling beyond the QR code: The QR/barcode document must carry batch, product, dates, size, quantity, ingredients (with manufacturer and lot), and a COA download link, plus the on-package batch number, website, expiration date, milligrams of extract, manufacturer, and 0.3% statement (IC 24-4-21-4(a), quoted above). Proposed additional bans on misleading packaging and unauthorised trademarks appeared in 2023 SB 293 and 2024 HB 1079 drafts; those drafts did not become law as presented here, so they are not listed as current requirements.

1.5 Smokable hemp — what the 2019 law bans, the federal challenge, and what is enforced today

What SEA 516 (2019; P.L.190-2019) did: It aligned Indiana’s hemp definition with the 2018 Farm Bill, created the commercial grower/handler licensing system, added the hemp bud/flower definitions, added the smokable-hemp definition, and added IC 35-48-4-10.1. Signed/approved 2 May 2019 (P.L.190-2019 header: “[S.519. Approved May 2, 2019]” in the enrolled-act PDF — note the enrolled act number in that PDF is cited as S.519 in one header line; the commonly cited Senate Enrolled Act number for the hemp bill is SEA 516. Enrolled-act number conflict — Verify on iga before citing SEA 516 vs. the P.L.190-2019 header in a filing.)

IC 35-48-4-10.1(a) — dealing in smokable hemp, Class A misdemeanor. “A person who: (1) knowingly or intentionally: (A) manufactures; (B) finances the manufacture of; (C) delivers; (D) finances the delivery of; or (E) possesses; smokable hemp; or (2) possesses smokable hemp with intent to: (A) manufacture; (B) finance the manufacture of; (C) deliver; or (D) finance the delivery of; smokable hemp; commits dealing in smokable hemp, a Class A misdemeanor.” Subsection (b) exempts financial institutions and state/federal agencies from the financing prongs. Subsection (c), added by SEA 335 (P.L.142-2020, §81), provides: “Subsection (a)(1)(C), (a)(1)(D), (a)(1)(E), (a)(2)(C), and (a)(2)(D) do not apply to the shipment of smokable hemp from a licensed producer in another state in continuous transit through Indiana to a licensed handler in any state.” FindLaw IC 35-48-4-10.1, current as of 1 Jan 2026; LawServer IC 35-48-4-10.1, accessed 28 Oct 2026.

Separate civil/licensing restriction: IC 15-15-13-19 — hemp bud and hemp flower “may be sold only to a processor licensed under this chapter,” with a civil penalty under IC 15-15-13-13/20 for violation. In practice this means retail sale of raw flower to a consumer is not a lawful “hemp product” sale even before the criminal smokable-hemp offence is reached.

Federal court challenge — C.Y. Wholesale, Inc. v. Holcomb:

Date / stepWhat happenedSource
28 June 2019Complaint filed, U.S. District Court, Southern District of Indiana, No. 1:19-cv-02659, by Indiana hemp wholesalers/retailers (including the Midwest Hemp Council interests) challenging SEA 516’s smokable-hemp provisions on Farm Bill preemption grounds.Justia docket summary; Studicata case summary, accessed 28 Oct 2026.
13 Sept 2019District court granted a preliminary injunction against enforcement of SEA 516 as it related to criminalising possession, manufacture, financing, or delivery of smokable hemp, on preemption grounds.S.D. Ind. order discussed in Justia Doc. 48.
2020 (during appeal)SEA 335 enacted, adding the continuous-transit exception in IC 35-48-4-10.1(c), in an apparent attempt to cure the interstate-transport preemption problem.Justia Doc. 48 discussion; P.L.142-2020, §81.
14 Apr 2020 / 8 July 2020Seventh Circuit heard oral argument 14 Apr 2020 and, on 8 July 2020, reversed the preliminary injunction as overly broad: C.Y. Wholesale, Inc. v. Holcomb, 965 F.3d 541 (7th Cir. 2020). The court expressly said its holding “should not be misunderstood as saying that a properly tailored injunction is not warranted,” because “[a] state cannot evade the Farm Law’s express preemption of laws prohibiting the interstate transportation of industrial hemp by criminalizing its possession and delivery.” The most that would have been warranted on express-preemption grounds was an injunction tailored to transit through the state, plus ancillary possession/delivery restrictions only to the extent they interfere with that transit.965 F.3d 541, 548–49, quoted in Justia Doc. 48 and Harris Sliwoski analysis, accessed 28 Oct 2026.
On remand (2021)Plaintiffs amended to challenge SEA 335 as well and sought a renewed preliminary injunction. The district court denied the renewed motion: plaintiffs had not shown standing for the express-preemption theory as framed (their shipments were to/from non-licensed producers) and had not shown likelihood of success on conflict preemption, because the Seventh Circuit had already rejected the argument that SEA 516 impermissibly altered the federal hemp definition or obstructed Congress’s overall intent.Justia Doc. 48; JD Supra summary of the remand decision, accessed 28 Oct 2026. Final merits judgment / dismissal terms Verify on the official docket (PACER / Justia docket) — no final merits judgment was located in this run and none is asserted here.

What is enforced today: The smokable-hemp ban is in the Code and is not under a current preliminary injunction as found in this run. Possession, manufacture, financing, and delivery of smokable hemp in Indiana (outside the licensed-producer continuous-transit exception) remain a Class A misdemeanor under IC 35-48-4-10.1, and retail sale of bud/flower to anyone other than a licensed processor remains restricted by IC 15-15-13-19. Shops openly selling hemp flower in Indiana are therefore selling a product Indiana law treats as unlawful at retail, even if the same flower is lawful in the state of origin. Enforcement intensity by county is not published in a central dashboard — Unavailable for county-by-county figures in this run.

Proposed repeals that did NOT become law: 2023 SB 293 and 2024 HB 1079 drafts would have repealed IC 35-48-1-26.6 and IC 35-48-4-10.1 and replaced smokable hemp with “craft hemp flower.” Neither draft is current law. Do not cite those drafts as a repeal.

1.6 Intoxicating hemp — delta-8, delta-9, delta-10, THCA, and other hemp-derived THC

Statutory starting point: A product qualifies as low-THC hemp extract only if it (1) derives from hemp, (2) is ≤0.3% total delta-9 THC including precursors by weight, and (3) “contains no other controlled substances” (IC 35-48-1-17.5(a)(3)). The entire intoxicating-hemp dispute turns on prong (3): if a THC variant is itself a Schedule I controlled substance under Indiana law, the product cannot be low-THC hemp extract even if its delta-9 content is ≤0.3%.

Attorney General Official Opinion 2023-1 (12 January 2023, Attorney General Todd Rokita). This is an official AG opinion, not statute and not a court ruling. It does not bind courts and, by its own terms, “cannot opine on the charging or prosecution of individual cases and defers to the prosecuting attorneys and law enforcement officers.” Its conclusion, verbatim:

“Delta-8 THC and other THC variants, as well as designer cannabinoids are Schedule I controlled substances pursuant to Ind. Code § 35-48-2-4(d)(31). Delta-8 THC, delta-10 THC, and THCP are naturally found in the cannabis plant but in trace amounts, and most of the products on the market are at least partially – if not fully – synthetically derived. Ind. Code § 35-48-2-4(d)(31) not only declares synthetic derivatives of all cannabis species as Schedule I controlled substances, but also the extracts, isomers, and any other derivatives. There are very limited exceptions for substances with concentrations below 0.3% delta-9 THC, but none of these apply to the compounds discussed in this Opinion. THCO is not found naturally in the cannabis plant, so it is always a synthetic substance and therefore is unquestionably a Schedule I controlled substance under Ind. Code § 35-48-2-4(d)(31). The AIA does not preempt state law in the regulation of hemp, so Indiana can classify these THC variants as Schedule I controlled substances without conflicting with federal law.” Official Opinion 2023-1 (in.gov PDF), pp. 13–14 (conclusion), accessed 28 Oct 2026.

What the opinion covers, product by product:

Product / variantPosition under current Indiana law as found
Delta-8 THCAG: Schedule I. Retail sale is therefore, on the AG’s reading, outside low-THC hemp extract protection. In practice widely sold; prosecutions are county-prosecutor decisions. No definitive Indiana appellate ruling that delta-8 is or is not a controlled substance was located — see Dickens below.
Delta-10 THC, THCPAG: Schedule I, same reasoning as delta-8.
THC-O (THCO)AG: “unquestionably” Schedule I, because it is always synthetic and not found naturally in the plant.
THCA (tetrahydrocannabinolic acid)No AG opinion section singling out THCA was located in this run. THCA is a precursor to delta-9 THC, and the low-THC definition counts precursors toward the 0.3% total. A non-smokable THCA product that, on a total-THC (post-decarboxylation) basis, exceeds 0.3% therefore fails prong (2). A THCA product within 0.3% total and with no other controlled substance would, on the statute’s text, be within low-THC hemp extract — but smokable THCA flower is separately barred by the smokable-hemp / bud-flower rules. The federal total-THC definition after 11 Dec 2026 (see below) will treat THCA the same way federally. Verify current OISC total-THC testing protocol for THCA.
Delta-9 THC in hemp (≤0.3%)Lawful as low-THC hemp extract if all three prongs and the labelling/testing rules are met. Above 0.3%, it is marijuana / a controlled substance, regardless of “hemp-derived” labelling.
HHC, HHCP, and other semi-synthetic cannabinoidsNo Indiana statute naming HHC/HHCP as such was located. The AG’s “other THC variants / designer cannabinoids” reasoning would, if accepted by a court, cover them; no court ruling to that effect was located. Treat as Verify / high-risk, not as clearly lawful or clearly banned by statute.
CBD, CBG, CBN (non-intoxicating, no other controlled substance)Within low-THC hemp extract if ≤0.3% total delta-9 and labelling/testing rules are met. No AG opinion in this run treats CBD itself as Schedule I.

Court test — State v. Devin A. Dickens, 24A-CR-1912 (Ind. Ct. App. 6 May 2025). The Court of Appeals did not decide whether delta-8 is a controlled substance. It held only that a charging information alleging possession / possession with intent to deliver “THC, a controlled substance, pure or adulterated, classified in Schedule I” (citing IC 35-48-4-2(a)(2) and IC 35-48-4-7(a)(1)) was sufficient on its face, because low-THC hemp extract and controlled substance are mutually exclusive classifications and the State need not plead the negative (that the substance was not low-THC hemp extract). Dickens’s petition to transfer to the Indiana Supreme Court (filed 28 July 2025, arguing plain statutory language makes delta-8 not a controlled substance) was located in secondary filing excerpts; its disposition was Verify on the Indiana Supreme Court docket and is not asserted here. A separate federal challenge to the AG opinion (S.D. Ind. No. 1:23-cv-01115) was dismissed 18 Mar 2025, Doc. 152, Order on Cross-Motions for Summary Judgment, for want of subject-matter jurisdiction in substance because the opinion is not binding and is not law and the plaintiffs showed no redressable injury against the Attorney General alone, without prejudice to re-filing in state court — that dismissal is not a ruling that the products are lawful.

THC beverages: No Indiana statute creates a lawful hemp-THC beverage category, potency cap, serving size, or on-premises consumption regime. A beverage that qualifies as low-THC hemp extract (≤0.3% total delta-9, no other controlled substance, labelling/COA) is within that category; a beverage with intoxicating delta-8/delta-9 above that threshold, or with a variant the AG treats as Schedule I, is not. THC “mocktails,” taproom seltzers, and similar products sold in Indianapolis and elsewhere (reported in 2026 trade press) operate in the same grey area, not under a beverage licence. No Indiana THC-beverage tax, licence, or serving rule was located.

State response to the new federal hemp definition and the 12 Nov / 11 Dec 2026 dates: None enacted as of 28 Oct 2026. No Indiana statute, rule, or OISC guidance mirroring PL 119-37 §781 or PL 119-103 §2019 was located. The only state vehicle that would have done so — 2026 SB 250, with an effective date mirroring the federal 12 Nov 2026 date (except minor-access provisions) — died (Part 1 §10). After 11 Dec 2026, therefore, the federal definition (total THC, 0.4 mg total THC per container cap for finished products, as supplied in the baseline) will apply as federal law, while Indiana’s state statutes will still say 0.3% delta-9 / low-THC hemp extract, with no state 0.4 mg cap and no state retail regime. How Indiana prosecutors, OISC, and courts reconcile that conflict is unresolved and flagged for Run 2 / 2027-session follow-up; do not present either “federal law automatically rewrites Indiana law” or “Indiana law immunises a federally non-compliant product” as settled.

Minimum age for hemp-derived THC: None in current state law (see §1.4). Proposed 21+ rules died. Retailer voluntary 21+ policies are not law.

1.7 Packaging, labelling, advertising, delivery, zoning, and buffer rules for hemp products

  • Packaging/labelling (current, finished low-THC hemp extract): IC 24-4-21-4(a) as quoted in §1.4 — QR/barcode document, batch number, website, expiration date (≤2 years from manufacture), milligrams of extract, manufacturer, 0.3% statement. Violation = Class B infraction (Class A if prior unrelated judgment), IC 24-4-21-5.
  • Child-resistant packaging: No current IC 24-4-21 child-resistant requirement was located. Child-resistant packaging appears only in proposed SB 478 / SB 250 texts. Do not present it as current law.
  • Advertising: No hemp-specific advertising statute was located in current law — but a general advertising ban that directly covers marijuana and Schedule I products was enacted and must be read alongside it: HB 1390 (2025), Public Law 227-2025, added IC 24-4-26 (Advertising of Illegal Products), effective upon passage, prohibiting advertising by any medium of (1) a product containing marijuana or (2) a Schedule I controlled substance, with contract grandfathering for contracts entered into or renewed before the chapter's effective date, enforced by the Attorney General only (injunction, up to $5,000 first / $10,000 second / $15,000 each additional violation, plus reasonable costs, penalties to the state general fund). Advertising is not added as a deceptive consumer sales act. General Indiana deceptive-sales law (IC 24-5-0.5) and FDA/FTC rules apply in the background, but no hemp-specific health-claim ban or youth-targeting ban was found; the IC 24-4-26 ban above is the advertising ban that applies. Proposed SB 250 advertising restrictions (no false/misleading ads, no overconsumption promotion, no depiction of consumption, no depiction of persons under 21, no health/medical claims) died with the bill.
  • Delivery / online sales: No current state ban on online or delivery sales of qualifying low-THC hemp extract was located. Proposed SB 250 would have prohibited online/delivery sales of hemp-derived cannabinoid products; it died. Interstate shipment of hemp itself is governed by IC 15-15-13-11 (transport by handler licensee/designee/agent with licensing documents) and the federal interstate-transport protection; shipment of smokable hemp is subject to the continuous-transit exception only.
  • Zoning / buffers for hemp products: No hemp-specific state zoning or buffer distance was located. The only state buffer that touches hemp-selling shops is indirect: if the shop is a “tobacco and vaping business” (primary activity sale of tobacco/e-cigarettes/e-liquids and accessories), IC 35-46-1-11.2 / -11.4 prohibit operation within 1,000 feet of a public/private elementary or secondary school (measured nearest point to nearest point of a building used for instructional purposes), with grandfathering for businesses operating before the statutory dates; violation is a Class C misdemeanor. A 2026 proposal (HB 1402) to extend that distance to 3,520 feet remained in committee and is not law. Local zoning for retail shops is in Part 3.
  • Food adulteration: Proposed bills (SB 293, SB 478) would have provided that a food is not adulterated for containing low-THC hemp extract (or craft hemp flower). No such provision was confirmed as current law in this run — Verify IC 16-42-1/-2 current text. FDA’s position that CBD/THC cannot lawfully be added to foods in interstate commerce or marketed as dietary supplements is federal background, not an Indiana statute.

1.8 Seed-to-sale / product tracking

No statewide seed-to-sale or finished-product tracking system for hemp products exists in current Indiana law as found. What does exist is crop-level control: OISC licences with GPS coordinates and legal descriptions (IC 15-15-13-7), handler transport documents with grower/handler licence numbers (IC 15-15-13-11), seller receipts with seller name, place of business, licence number, and quantity (IC 15-15-13-18, buyer to retain for 2 years), pre-harvest sampling/testing and crop detention/seizure/embargo for over-threshold crops (IC 15-15-13-9(d)), and batch-level COA/labelling for finished low-THC hemp extract (IC 24-4-21-3, -4). Those are record-keeping and batch-testing duties, not a Metrc-style seed-to-sale database, and no tracking-system vendor or database was located for Indiana. Proposed SB 250 / SB 478 tracking and ATC registration systems died.

1.9 Employment and drug-testing protections

No cannabis-specific employment or drug-testing protection statute was located for Indiana. No statute was found that protects an employee for lawful hemp/CBD use, prohibits an employer from testing for THC, or creates a medical-cannabis employment protection (there is no medical programme to protect). General Indiana employment law (at-will employment, employer drug-testing policies, and safety-sensitive testing regimes) therefore governs, subject to federal DOT and other sector rules where they apply. This is a negative finding — Verify for any sector-specific rule (e.g. public safety, DOT) before advising an employer or employee. Interim-study testimony in 2023–2024 discussed employer testing in the context of a future adult-use market, but no protection was enacted.

1.10 Pending legislation — confirmed status, session note

Session structure: Indiana has a part-time, biennial-budget General Assembly: a long session in odd-numbered years (typically January–April, budget session) and a short session in even-numbered years (typically January–early March, non-budget, with earlier committee and chamber deadlines). A bill that does not pass both chambers by sine die in its session dies and must be reintroduced. That is why the February 2026 deadlines mattered for SB 250 and why “died in committee” in 2025 does not carry over to 2026. Interim study committees meet between sessions; they can study and recommend but cannot enact law.

2.1 IRS §280E and Indiana decoupling

Federal: Under the supplied baseline, §280E is generally lifted for Schedule III medical activity and remains fully applicable to adult-use activity. Indiana has no state-licensed medical activity, so no Indiana marijuana business currently benefits from that lift, and any adult-use activity in Indiana is both federally Schedule I and state-illegal.

Indiana conformity: Indiana’s adjusted gross income starts from federal AGI with Indiana modifications (IC 6-3-1-3.5). A secondary multistate tax table states: “IN — Yes. Ind. Code § 6-3-1-3.5(b); Ind. Code § 6-3-1-11(a). Indiana conforms to IRC §280E to the extent of conformity to the underlying federal deduction or credit.” Maryland General Assembly testimony table (multistate §280E conformity chart), mgaleg PDF, accessed 28 Oct 2026 — secondary source, used only for the conformity proposition and marked Verify against current IC 6-3-1-3.5. No Indiana §280E decoupling statute for cannabis was located. For lawful hemp businesses, §280E does not apply in the first place, because hemp is not a Schedule I/II controlled substance and hemp businesses are not “trafficking” in such substances; their ordinary business expenses are therefore not disallowed by §280E as a matter of federal law (subject to normal deductibility rules). Do not apply §280E to a lawful hemp/CBD business, and do not apply a decoupling benefit to a non-existent Indiana medical business.

2.2 Revenue figures (official only)

  • OISC hemp fees FY 2025: $55,050 total ($52,500 licences + $3,550 other fees by arithmetic from the LSA note’s $55,050 total and $52,500 licence subtotal — the $3,550 remainder is derived, not separately stated in the note excerpt).
  • No official Indiana cannabis tax revenue exists (no legal market, no excise).
  • No official Indiana hemp/CBD retail sales total, hemp product tax revenue total, or DOR hemp-category revenue figure was located. Any “Indiana hemp market size” dollar figure in secondary sources is not an official revenue figure and is not presented as one in Part 6.
  • No official estimate of tax revenue forgone from cross-border marijuana purchases was located.

PART 3: LOCAL RULES

3.1 What authority counties, cities, towns, and townships have — and what state law preempts

Home rule, with express limits. Indiana is a home-rule state in form: IC 36-1-3 gives units (counties, municipalities, townships) “all powers that they need for the effective operation of government as to local affairs,” including powers not expressly granted by statute. But IC 36-1-3-8 withholds specific powers, including — in IC 36-1-3-8(a)(7) — “the power to regulate conduct that is regulated by a state agency, except as expressly granted by statute.” Indiana courts have applied that limit to strike local ordinances that impose duties on activities regulated by a state agency or prohibit conduct authorised under a state licence/permit (see, e.g., the discussion in Town of Avon v. West Central Conservancy District and related home-rule cases, summarised in secondary planning-law sources). A local unit therefore cannot use home rule to override a state hemp licence or to re-regulate a subject the state has comprehensively regulated, unless a statute expressly grants that local power.

Hemp-specific preemption — the clearest rule in this Part. IC 15-15-13-21 provides, verbatim:

“A county, city, town, or township may not adopt or enforce an ordinance restricting or regulating: (1) the growth, production, or processing of hemp; or (2) any subject regulated by this chapter.” OISC statute PDF, IC 15-15-13-21, added P.L.190-2019, §22.

That is a hemp-production preemption. It bars a county/city/town/township from banning or specially regulating hemp growing, production, or processing, and from regulating any subject already regulated by IC 15-15-13. It does not, on its face, preempt general retail zoning, general business licensing, or tobacco/vaping-shop regulation for a retail shop that happens to sell finished hemp products, because finished-product retail is not itself regulated by IC 15-15-13 (OISC itself notes it does not regulate finished hemp products for retail purchase). That distinction — production/processing preempted, retail-shop land use generally local — is the correct frame, and it is why Part 3 finds retail zoning cases (Indianapolis BZA, McCordsville smoke-shop definition) alongside a production preemption.

Who does what, in practice:

LevelWhat it can do for hemp / CBD / smoke-vape shopsWhat it cannot do (as found)
County (including county plan commission, IC 36-7-4)Adopt a comprehensive plan and zoning ordinance for unincorporated areas; regulate land use, setbacks, and use categories through zoning; enforce general health/safety and business rules authorised by statute.Adopt/enforce an ordinance restricting/regulating hemp growth, production, processing, or any IC 15-15-13 subject (IC 15-15-13-21). Cannot legalise marijuana, create a medical programme, or override state criminal law.
City / town (including consolidated city-county Indianapolis/Marion County)Same zoning/planning power within corporate limits (Indianapolis via its Consolidated Zoning and Subdivision Ordinance and Board of Zoning Appeals); general business registration/licensing where authorised; smoking ordinances within state law (IC 7.1-5-12 statewide smoking ban and its exemptions).Same IC 15-15-13-21 preemption. Cannot ban hemp production/processing by ordinance. Cannot create a local marijuana market.
TownshipTownships in Indiana have limited, enumerated powers (township assistance, fire protection, cemetery, etc. under IC 36-6). They do not have a general zoning/planning power comparable to counties/cities, and no township hemp/shop zoning ordinance was located.Cannot adopt a hemp-production ordinance (IC 15-15-13-21 expressly names townships) and, as found, has no general retail-zoning power to use for smoke shops. Verify for any township with a special plan-commission delegation.
State (for contrast)OISC/seed commissioner: hemp grower/handler licensing. ATC: tobacco/vaping sales certificates and enforcement. DOR: RRMC and sales tax. ISP / local prosecutors: criminal enforcement of IC 35-48.—

Proposed broader preemption that did NOT become law: 2026 HB 1416 would have created IC 36-1-3.3, barring municipalities/counties from regulating conduct in fields already covered by specified Indiana Code titles (including natural resources, agriculture, business/trade, professions) unless expressly authorised, with a private cause of action. It remained in committee and is not law. Do not cite it as a current preemption rule.

3.2 Local zoning ordinances, moratoriums, caps, bans, and medical-cannabis resolutions

No statewide hemp-specific local ban, cap, or moratorium was located. The findings below are the specific local items actually located in this run; absence of an item for a jurisdiction below means “no such ordinance was located in the sources searched,” not “the jurisdiction has affirmatively authorised the use.” Local codes change by ordinance and by BZA variance, so any address-specific decision requires a check of that jurisdiction’s current code and BZA docket.

  • McCordsville, Hancock County (town) — smoke-shop definition including hemp/CBD. Ordinance No. 041123D (Town Council document, via Revize) defines “Smoke Shop” as “A retail shop specializing in the selling of tobacco products, glassware, and/or hemp products, like CBD. They may also include vape products, as defined in this Article.” A “Cigar Shop” definition in the same ordinance prohibits hemp products (including CBD) and vape/e-cigarette products as more than incidental sales. The ordinance number suggests an April 2023 adoption date, but the exact adoption/effective date was not verified from the ordinance text in this run — Verify date and current code section. This is a definition / use-classification, not a hemp ban: it channels hemp/CBD retail into the “Smoke Shop” use category rather than the “Cigar Shop” category.
  • Indianapolis / Marion County (consolidated city-county) — BZA variance case, not a ban. Board of Zoning Appeals Division II, 8 July 2025, Case 2025-UV2-009, 3889 West Perry Street, Decatur Township, Council District #21, zoned D-5: a variance of use/development standards “to provide for the operation of a hemp, CBD, health & wellness, and smoking paraphernalia retail business (not permitted)” with parking variances was listed as “Petitioner to withdraw.” Indianapolis-Marion County BZA agenda, 8 July 2025 BZA Division II, accessed 28 Oct 2026. The lesson is land-use, not criminal: in that D-5 residential district, that retail use was “not permitted” as of right and needed a use variance, which was withdrawn. It does not establish a citywide hemp-shop ban. The staff report adds that the petitioner informed staff of intent to withdraw and refile to pursue rezoning to C-3 — the withdrawal must not be presented as a BZA denial.
  • Fort Wayne, Allen County — smoking / cigar-lounge ordinance, not a hemp ban. Bill No. G-25-11-41 (Dec 2025, Fort Wayne Common Council documents) proposes to amend Fort Wayne Municipal Code Chapter 95 — committee action 9 Dec 2025 was 'Do Pass' and final adoption and mayoral approval stay Verify on the Fort Wayne clerk's record, so it must not yet be called an adopted ordinance; it would amend Chapter 95 (Health, Safety, and Sanitation, smoking regulation) to add a “Cigar Lounge” definition/exemption tracking the state exemption in IC 7.1-5-12. No hemp-specific ban, cap, or moratorium was located in that bill. Fort Wayne G-25-11-41 PDF.
  • Statewide smoking law (background for lounges/shops): IC 7.1-5-12 prohibits smoking in most public places/workplaces, with exemptions for specified businesses (including qualifying cigar bars/lounges, tobacco retail, etc. meeting state criteria). A hemp/CBD retail shop is not, by selling hemp, a “cigar lounge” or otherwise exempt; on-premises smoking/consumption in a shop would need to fit a state exemption, which was not located for hemp lounges. The 2026 trade-press report of a Fountain Square (Indianapolis) THC “consumption lounge” operating in a grey area is secondary and does not establish a lawful lounge category.
  • Medical-cannabis resolutions: No official county/city/town resolution supporting or opposing a state medical cannabis programme was located in this run for the Part 3 jurisdictions. Local “support” statements in advocacy press are not ordinances and are not presented as such. Unavailable for a compiled resolution list.

Out-of-state look-alikes excluded: Search results for “smoke shop moratorium” that returned Bristol (Connecticut), Harlingen (Texas), Riverside (California), and similar ordinances were not used, because they are not Indiana jurisdictions. The same-name warning in the brief applies here: a moratorium in another state’s city with a similar name is not an Indiana ordinance.

3.3 Official list of jurisdictions with such ordinances — and jurisdictions checked

There is no official statewide list. No OISC, ATC, DOR, or General Assembly list of counties/cities/towns with hemp, CBD, or smoke/vape-shop ordinances was located. OISC publishes hemp licence information (Part 5), not local ordinances. Local ordinances, where they exist, live in each jurisdiction’s code (Municode, American Legal Publishing, or the clerk’s office) and in BZA/plan-commission dockets.

Kokomo / Howard CountyNo hemp-specific ban/cap/moratorium located in sources searched.
Anderson / Madison CountyNo hemp-specific ban/cap/moratorium located in sources searched. (Anderson, IN is not Anderson, SC.)
Elkhart / Elkhart CountyNo hemp-specific ban/cap/moratorium located in sources searched.
Michigan City / LaPorte CountyNo hemp-specific ban/cap/moratorium located in sources searched.
McCordsville / Hancock County (additional located example)Smoke Shop definition including hemp/CBD; Cigar Shop incidental-sales prohibition (see §3.2). Date Verify.

For each “no ordinance located” row, the search was a web/code search for that jurisdiction plus hemp/CBD/smoke-shop zoning/moratorium terms on 28 Oct 2026. That is not a certified search of every municipal code supplement or BZA docket, and it does not prove that no variance, special exception, or unpublished enforcement practice exists. Address-specific work must check the current code and the plan-commission/BZA docket for that address.

3.4 Local business licence / permit fees and local buffer rules

  • General business registration: Indiana businesses register with the Secretary of State via INBiz and obtain an RRMC from DOR ($25/location, Part 2). Those are state, not local, fees. No separate statewide “hemp shop” local licence fee exists in current law.
  • Local general business licences: Some Indiana cities/towns require a general business licence or registration, but no uniform local fee schedule for hemp/CBD shops was located, and no jurisdiction in the Part 3 list was found to publish a hemp-specific local licence fee. Unavailable as a compiled fee table; check the clerk’s office for the specific city/town.
  • Tobacco sales certificate (state, ATC): A business selling tobacco/e-cigarette products needs an ATC tobacco sales certificate (renewed every 3 years per the ATC application form). The certificate fee is $200 for a three-year certificate (ATC State Form 51357, rev. 6-26) — and the certificate is for tobacco/vaping, not for hemp as such.
  • Local buffers: No hemp-specific local buffer distance (from schools, parks, churches, other shops) was located for any Part 3 jurisdiction. The operative buffer that can affect a hemp-selling shop is the state tobacco/vaping buffer: 1,000 feet from a public/private elementary or secondary school for a “tobacco and vaping business” (IC 35-46-1-11.2 / -11.4), measured nearest point to nearest point of a building used for instructional purposes, Class C misdemeanor for violation, with grandfathering. Local zoning may impose additional setbacks through a use variance or a specific zoning district, but no published hemp-shop buffer table was located.

3.5 Business concentration — official sources only

Official sources do not publish a ranked cluster map of hemp retailers. What official sources do say is limited to cultivation licensing: OISC’s 2024 report states 54 licences were issued in 41 Indiana counties (grower 24 in 20 counties, grower/handler 8 in 8 counties, handler 22 in 13 counties — county names are not given in the report excerpt located). Purdue Extension (May 2025) states 21 licensed growers in the 2025 season, without a county ranking. No official source in this run ranks cities or counties by hemp retail concentration, and this file does not estimate or rank clusters. The secondary Whitney Economics estimate (537 retail outlets + 1,383 gas stations selling cannabinoid products, 2023) is a statewide estimate, not a county/city concentration ranking, and is reported in Part 6 as secondary only.

PART 4: LICENCE TYPES

4.1 State cannabis licences — none

Indiana issues no cannabis licences. There is no medical cannabis licence, adult-use licence, dispensary licence, cultivation licence for marijuana, processing licence for marijuana, testing licence for marijuana, or transport licence for marijuana in current Indiana law. Any “cannabis licence” offered, sold, or claimed in Indiana is not an Indiana state licence. Hemp licences below are not cannabis licences and do not authorise marijuana activity.
