4.3 Licence types proposed in pending medical cannabis bills — PROPOSED ONLY

All of the following are PROPOSED and not law. They are listed only to answer the brief’s question about what pending bills would have created.

  • HB 1178 (2025) — PROPOSED: Medical marijuana programme permits for cultivation, processing, testing, transportation, and dispensing, implemented/enforced by the Indiana Department of Health (state department), with diversion-prevention, labelling, child-resistant packaging, and independent testing requirements. No fee schedule is asserted here (fiscal note states an effective date of 1 July 2025 for the bill, which never became law).
  • SB 286 (2026) — PROPOSED: Medical + adult-use cannabis commission licensing framework (cultivation, processing, retail, etc. as described in the introduced text’s advisory-committee and programme sections), plus a cannabis research and development programme (IC 7.1-9-5). No licence fees or caps are asserted here; the introduced text excerpt located does not support a complete fee table.
  • SB 478 (2025) — PROPOSED (craft hemp, not marijuana): Craft hemp flower retail-dealer certificate (in the 2023 SB 293 predecessor, a retail dealer’s certificate from DOR in addition to an RRMC, plus an 8% excise — — see audit: the House-engrossed 2025 SB 478 also proposed ATC craft hemp manufacturer and retail permits (retail capped at 20,000), a 15% craft hemp flower product excise tax effective 1 January 2026, and a DOR retail dealer's certificate, all PROPOSED ONLY and died in conference; the 2023 8% excise is a separate earlier proposal (its excise provisions were amended out before that bill died), and neither is law). 2025 SB 478 also proposed handler work-in-progress extract rules and craft-flower testing/packaging rules.
  • SB 250 (2026) — PROPOSED (hemp-derived cannabinoids, not marijuana): ATC manufacturer, distributor, retail, and carrier permits for hemp-derived cannabinoid products, with packaging/labelling/testing, spot testing, a products fund, advertising restrictions, and criminal penalties. A secondary trade report mentions a $550 retailer permit in the SB 250 context; that fee is Verify against the enrolled/committee text and is not a current fee. House draft amendments (AM025007/AM025008) restructured, rather than simply removing, the permit scheme as craft hemp retail permits capped at 20,000 with a 1,000-foot school-location rule; whether any House amendment was adopted is unconfirmed and no House version passed — another reason the fee cannot be presented as settled even as a proposal.

4.4 Social equity

No social equity programme, current or proposed, was located for Indiana cannabis or hemp in this run. No statute, rule, or pending bill in the 2025–2026 lists searched creates a social-equity licence, priority, fee waiver, expungement-linked licensing benefit, or equity fund for cannabis/hemp in Indiana. (Expungement for marijuana offences generally is a Run 2, Part 12 topic and is not a licensing social-equity programme.) If a future bill adds an equity provision, it will need to be added as a new finding, not read into SB 286, HB 1178, SB 478, or SB 250 as searched.

These are illustrative, non-official estimates only. They are not quotes, not OISC/ATC/DOR figures, and not a business plan. Actual costs vary by county, lease, build-out, inventory, and whether the business also holds an OISC grower/handler licence. The only official figures in the table are the OISC and RRMC fees; everything else is marked as an estimate range for planning discussion and must be verified with vendors, landlords, labs, and counsel before any commitment.

Note the apparent difference between “60 licences in FY 2025” and “21 growers in the 2025 season”: the FY figure is a licence count (including handlers and combined licences) for the fiscal year, while the Purdue figure is a grower count for the growing season as it was getting underway. Interactive dashboard vs. download summary: OISC’s licence system is an interactive, account-based portal with no public download option located. INBiz is an interactive search with no bulk hemp download. The downloadable files verified in this Part are four PDFs (2024 Hemp Report, 2023 seed-supplier list, USDA Indiana Hemp Plan, plus the OISC grower/handler contacts directory file hemp_grower_handler_contacts_032725.pdf, dated 27 Mar 2025 — a directory-opt-in list only, not a complete licence list; holder contact details from it are not reproduced in this report). No export/download button for a live licence-holder list was located; if OISC has an export function inside the licensee portal, it is not a public export and is not described here as one.

The decline from the 2020 press figure (280 growers / 8,900 acres) to the 2024–2025 official figures (54–60 licences; 21 growers / 387 acres in season) is stark, but this file does not attribute it to a single cause. Secondary analyses cite the national CBD oversupply / price collapse after 2019–2020, processing bottlenecks, and the shift to intoxicating derivatives as context; those are secondary explanations, not OISC findings, and are not presented as official Indiana causes.

6.2 Hemp product market size and retailer counts

  • Official Indiana hemp product market size ($): Unavailable — no DOR, OISC, ISDA, or USDA Indiana-specific retail market-size dollar figure was located.
  • Official retailer count: Unavailable — no state registry of hemp retailers exists (Part 4), so no official retailer count exists.
  • Secondary retailer / jobs estimate (dated, not official): Whitney Economics, Indiana Hemp-Derived Cannabinoid Summary (public-facing summary dated 20 Oct 2023 (the '10-31-23' in the file name is not the document date), via Indiana Capital Chronicle PDF): an estimated 537 retail outlets plus approximately 1,383 gas stations (out of 3,069) selling cannabinoid products — survey-based industry research, not an official count: the Indiana survey was sent to about 600 businesses and drew only 36 responses, with a stated 90% confidence level and greater-than-10% margin of error; the same summary also gives about $637 million retail sales revenue, $1.78 billion total economic impact, and $44.6 million retail-sales tax revenue; total cannabinoid-related jobs 11,017 (6,315 retail-store jobs at 11.76 employees per retail operator + 4,702 gas-station jobs at 3.4 per operator); retail wages “over $388 million” (summary wording: “between 6,300 and 11,000 workers earning over $388 million in wages”). Whitney Economics summary PDF, accessed 28 Oct 2026. This is a survey-based industry estimate from 2023, not a licence count, and it predates the 2024–2026 legislative and federal changes. Do not use it as a 2026 count.
  • National CBD market figures (e.g. Market Research Future: US CBD hemp oil market $2.22bn in 2024, $2.56bn in 2025) are not Indiana figures and are not used as Indiana market size here.

6.3 Indiana residents buying in neighbouring legal states — official figures first

No official Indiana figure was located for the number of Indiana residents buying marijuana in Michigan, Illinois, or Ohio, the volume they buy, or the tax revenue Indiana “loses” to those states. Secondary press (e.g. Evansville Courier & Press, 24 Nov 2025, “Here’s how much Indiana’s neighbors are making off marijuana sales”) reports neighbours’ adult-use sales totals, not an Indiana-resident purchase estimate, and is not used here as an Indiana estimate. Any cross-border purchase estimate would require neighbouring states’ out-of-state customer data or an Indiana survey, neither of which was located as an official source in this run. This is therefore Unavailable for Run 1, and is flagged as a Run 2 follow-up only if a neighbouring state publishes a residency breakdown (see also Part 10 in Run 2).

6.4 Official price data

None located. No OISC, ISDA, USDA, or DOR Indiana hemp biomass, CBD, or hemp-product price series was located in this run. Do not use national biomass price anecdotes as Indiana prices.

6.5 Cannabis/hemp-friendly banks and credit unions operating in Indiana

No official list was located. There is no Indiana state list of banks/credit unions that serve hemp businesses, and there is no federal cannabis banking law (baseline). Because hemp is lawful federally and in Indiana, hemp businesses are not, as a category, subject to the same banking exclusion as marijuana businesses, but individual banks’ policies vary and are not published in a central Indiana list. No bank or credit union is named here as “hemp-friendly” without a source, and none should be inferred from the absence of a list. Verify with the institution and counsel for any specific account.

6.6 Industry events and trade shows in Indiana

No official Indiana cannabis/hemp event calendar was located. That does not mean Indiana hemp events do not happen — documented historical/recurring events include: Hemp at the Crossroads Education and Trade Show, Indianapolis, 16-17 Nov 2019 (Marriott East, Midwest Hemp Council with the Indiana Farmers Union hemp chapter, with a trade show; a further edition was advertised for 1-3 Dec 2021 at the Embassy Suites, Plainfield); Purdue Hemp Field Day, 30 July 2025 (Agronomy Center for Research and Education, West Lafayette) and an earlier field day 19 July 2019 (Meigs Purdue Ag Center, Lafayette); and the Indiana Horticulture Conference hemp track, 11-13 Feb 2020 (Indianapolis Marriott East). No current, dated calendar was located, so check Purdue Extension, the Midwest Hemp Council and ISDA pages for the current season before travelling. No OISC, ISDA, or Purdue event list for Indiana cannabis/hemp trade shows was found as a current, dated calendar in this run. Purdue Extension publishes hemp production articles and field research (e.g. the May 2025 hemp production article above), and the Midwest Hemp Council is an Indiana industry association referenced in legislative testimony, but neither was located as a dated Indiana trade-show listing in this run, so no event names, dates, or venues are asserted here. Unavailable / Verify — check Purdue Extension, the Midwest Hemp Council, and the Indiana State Department of Agriculture event pages for the current season before travelling to any event.

Run 1 scope reminder: Parts 7–12 (hemp licence detail, cannabis-zoned properties, regulators/history, tribal/interstate, operations rules, consumer/social rules) and the combined research notes for both runs are in Run 2, after the user replies “go.” Nothing in Parts 0–6 above should be read as a Run 2 finding.

Run 1 file: Indiana cannabis & hemp, Parts 0–6. Built 28 October 2026. This is research, not legal advice. Statute text should be rechecked against the current official Indiana Code on iga.in.gov before any filing, licence application, or enforcement decision, particularly for the 2025 recodification of IC 35-48-1 to IC 35-48-1.1 and for any 2027-session change.

REWRITTEN REPORT — PARTS 7–12 (AUDITED)

PART 7: HEMP LICENCES

7.1 Indiana runs its own USDA-approved state plan — not USDA direct licensing

7.2 Every licence / permit type

Statutory base: IC 15-15-13. Scope limit in the statute itself: IC 15-15-13-1(b) (as amended by P.L.190-2019) provides that nothing in the chapter authorises the State Seed Commissioner to regulate a hemp product. That is why there is no finished-product licence below — it is a statutory limit, not an oversight.

IC 15-15-13-4, “grower”: “(1) an individual, a partnership, a company, or a corporation that produces hemp for commercial purposes; or (2) a person, as part of a hemp research program conducted by a state educational institution (as defined by IC 21-7-13-32).” IC 15-15-13-5, “handler”: “an individual, a partnership, a company, or a corporation that receives hemp for scientific research, or for processing into commodities, hemp products, or agricultural hemp seed.” IC 15-15-13-7(b): “All growers and handlers must have a hemp license issued by the state seed commissioner. Growers and handlers engaged in the production of agricultural hemp seed must also have an agricultural hemp seed production license.” OISC statute PDF, IC 15-15-13 (17 Dec 2021 compilation), accessed 28–29 Oct 2026.

Licence / permitAllowsDoes NOT allow / notesFee
1. Hemp grower licence (IC 15-15-13-7; Plan §33(1))Commercial hemp production, outdoor (fields) and indoor/roofed/contained (greenhouse, shade/slat house, cold frame, polyhouse, quonset hut, warehouse, hydroponics/grow-light rooms). Includes hemp grain grown for seed as oil/meal/flour/whole grain for consumption — expressly not licensed for sowing or for seed to produce a crop (Plan §33(1)(D)).A grower does not need a handler licence for grower activities. Growing without a licence, or at an unreported site, is treated as marijuana production in Indiana (plan late-fee rule). Smokable hemp remains banned — a grower licence does not authorise smokable-hemp sale.$750 collected (LSA); plan cap $1,000 (see §7.3)
2. Hemp handler licence (IC 15-15-13-7; Plan §33(2))Receiving hemp for scientific research or processing into commodities, hemp products, or agricultural hemp seed (statute). Plan wording is broader: persons who receive hemp “for any reason” other than as a grower — including transporting hemp in any quantity, processing, baling, harvesting, inspecting, brokering, testing / laboratory support, or receiving hemp for any purpose; a person who advertises or offers such services is a handler.Plan-vs-statute wording difference is noted, not resolved — do not present the Plan expansion as statute. Handler ≠ retailer: no finished-product retail permit exists. Hemp bud/flower may be sold only to a processor licensed under the chapter (IC 15-15-13-19(a)).$750 collected (LSA); plan cap $1,000
3. Grower/handler (combined holding)Both of the above, held by one person/entity.Not a third statutory licence type; it is how LSA/OISC count a person holding both licences (FY 2025: 10 grower-handler of 60 total). Fee is the sum of the two.$1,500 collected; plan cap $2,000
4. Agricultural hemp seed production licence (IC 15-15-13-7(b), -9(a)) — additional licence“An agricultural hemp seed production license … authorizes a grower or handler to produce and handle agricultural hemp seed for sale to licensed hemp growers and handlers” (IC 15-15-13-9(a)). Seller must ensure seed complies with State Seed Commissioner standards; Commissioner “shall make available to growers information that identifies sellers of agricultural hemp seed.” Plan §33(1)(C) lists seed production inside the grower category (sale of seed for sowing for fibre, grain, or cannabinoid-extraction crops; subject to federal seed code, Indiana seed laws, and Indiana Crop Improvement Association certification where applicable).Additional to, not a substitute for, the grower/handler licence (“must also have”). How OISC counts it in the 54/60 licence totals is Verify — LSA counts show only grower / handler / grower-handler, with no separate seed-production count.Verify — no separate fee in the LSA list; do not assume $750
5. Researcher licence (Plan §33(3); §§37–38) — plan/rule licence, not a separate IC 15-15-13 sectionResearch by persons “employed by institutions of higher education as defined in federal and state law.” Plan §37 (via search-index extract): researcher must be employed by an institution of higher education (IC 21-7-13-32), present and have approved in writing by the State Seed Commissioner a detailed research proposal and a restricted-size (not commercial-size) investigational site; may be exempted, subject to Commissioner approval, from minimum-acreage requirements and exempted or given reduced licence fees as determined by the Commissioner; background-check cost, if any, is the applicant’s responsibility.Not a commercial grower licence; research sites are restricted in size. A secondary seed-seller claim that the researcher licence “will go away, leaving only Grower and Processor” is unverified secondary, not adopted.Exempt or reduced, at Commissioner’s discretion; amount Verify
6. Hemp breeder licence / researcher-breeder (Plan §§50, 58–59, via extracts)Breeding and wild-Cannabis collection only where expressly licensed in writing: collection/sale/use of wild Cannabis is a violation “except for the expressed purposes and licensing by the state seed commissioner for hemp plant breeding, or hemp research by a person licensed under this article” (Plan §58(a)); minimum-quantity/minimum-area violations do not apply to university-employed researcher or licensed breeder activity (Plan §59(b)).Detail is thin in the extracts retrieved; whether “breeder” is a distinct licence or a researcher sub-type, and its fee, are Verify. Do not list it as a confirmed standalone licence type without that check.Verify
7. Handler licence to distribute plant clones (IC 15-15-13-9.5) — a handler-licence function, not a separate licence“A person who is a handler licensed under this section may distribute clones and other nonseed propagative materials of a hemp plant using the person’s own labeling” if the distributor: (1) complies with the chapter; (2) reports variety and quantity of each variety sold; (3) pays the inspection fee on the basis of the report; (4) labels as required by the Commissioner; (5) keeps records accurately determining named varieties and numbers distributed; (6) grants the Commissioner / authorised representative access to examine records and verify quantity and variety; (7) reports under oath, on Commissioner forms, each variety and quantity sold during each semiannual period; (8) any other information/conditions in the application. Added by P.L.190-2019, §11, effective upon passage. Revocation grounds include non-compliance, report >10 days late, false report, or labelling failure; a late fee is assessed if the inspection fee is >10 days late (§9.5(b)–(c)).Secondary summaries saying “handlers who distribute clones need their own licence” overstate §9.5: the section conditions a handler licence, it does not create a distinct “clone licence.”Inspection fee on a report basis; amount Verify
8. Seed distributor permit (IC 15-15-1-34, and new IC 15-15-1-34.5) — general Indiana Seed Law, not a hemp licenceIC 15-15-13-9(b): “A person who sells agricultural hemp seed to a grower must be a seed distributor who has a permit under IC 15-15-1-34.” Under §34, a distributor may apply for a permit to use its own labelling, report quantity sold, and pay the inspection fee on that report, agreeing to label as required, keep records, grant record-examination access, and report under oath on Commissioner forms; reports/fees are semiannual (periods ending 30 June and 31 December, due by the last day of the following month); late (>10 days) report/fee can lead to revocation and a 10% penalty on the unpaid inspection fee.This permit is about seed distribution generally; it does not authorise hemp cultivation or handling. See §7.4 for the 2026 change.See §7.4
Does NOT exist in IndianaNo cannabis licence of any kind (Run 1); no finished-product manufacturer, distributor, retailer, or carrier permit; no hemp retail licence; no low-THC-extract retail registration; no social-equity licence. The ATC manufacturer / distributor / retail / carrier permits in SB 250 (2026) and SB 478 (2025) were PROPOSED only and died (Run 1). Any list claiming a current Indiana “hemp retailer licence” or “ATC hemp permit” should be treated as describing a dead proposal or another state.—

7.3 Fees — statute vs. plan cap vs. amount actually collected (do not conflate these three)

  • Statute: IC 15-15-13-10: “The amount of any fees charged growers and handlers by the state seed commissioner under this chapter must be sufficient to cover the cost of the administration of this chapter, including the cost of conducting audits and testing.” (Added P.L.165-2014.) The statute sets no dollar amount. IC 15-15-13-7(c)(8) requires a “nonrefundable application fee, which must include the amount necessary to conduct a state or national criminal history background check, in an amount determined by the state seed commissioner.”
  • Plan cap (Plan §38(a), via extract): “Upon completion of an application for a license or renewal of a license for grower or handler license, a nonrefundable payment of not more than one thousand dollars ($1,000) shall be paid to the office of Indiana state seed commissioner. This fee is due for grower and handler applications, or not more than two thousand dollars ($2,000) for both.”
  • Amount actually collected (official, via LSA): grower $750; handler $750; grower-handler $1,500; site/change-of-record fee $50; remediation testing fee $325; FY 2025 total hemp-fee revenue $55,050 ($52,500 from licences). LSA SB 250 fiscal notes (FN001 / FN003 / COMH FN001), accessed 28–29 Oct 2026. The $50 and $325 figures are corroborated in the Plan itself: §38(c) $50 change-of-record fee; §38(d) $325 remediation sampling, travel, and laboratory fee.
  • Late fee (Plan §38(b)): “A grower or handler in possession of hemp, hemp clones, or agricultural hemp seed, without having an approved license issued by the state seed commissioner, or reporting a grow site location shall be assessed a late fee of seven hundred fifty dollars ($750) in addition to the required nonrefundable application fee to obtain a license. This fee shall not be counted as a civil penalty. This is a negligent violation.”
  • Other money provisions: IC 15-15-13-16 — all chapter fees are paid to the Purdue University Treasurer and expended by the Purdue Board of Trustees; the Dean of Agriculture reports receipts/expenditures to the Governor. IC 15-15-13-13(b) caps the Commissioner’s civil penalty at $2,500 per violation (plus lab-testing cost reimbursement, §13(e)); IC 15-15-13-20 provides up to $10,000 per violation for knowing/intentional licence/rule violations. LSA notes state no OISC civil penalties had been imposed (FY 2025 context) — read as that note’s statement, not a permanent claim.
  • Historical fee conflict, resolved in favour of the LSA figure for current law: Hemp Industry Daily (23 Oct 2020) reported applications costing “$1,000 a year” at plan approval. That matches the Plan cap, not the current collected amount. Do not quote $1,000 as the current fee.
  • Proposed fees that died: SB 250 as introduced would have added a $500 application fee, $100 optional ISP background-check fee, $10,000 annual fee per hemp licensee, and $1,000 negligent-violation fee (LSA FN001) — PROPOSED only, not current law. The later SB 250 permit-fee table (manufacturing $1,000 / distributing $750 / retail $550 / carrier $250 / application $250 / testing $250 / reinstatement $250) is likewise PROPOSED only.

7.4 Seed distributor permit — the 2026 change that must be rechecked

  • Pre-July 2026 mirror text (IC 15-15-1-34, Justia 2015 compilation and later mirrors): “(a) A distributor of agricultural seed in packages of not more than one (1) pound shall pay not later than January 15 of each year an annual fee of forty-five dollars ($45) for each brand of seed distributed” (retailers furnishing ≤1 lb from a properly labelled container on which the inspection fee has been paid are exempt). Added P.L.2-2008, amended P.L.75-2010.
  • 2026 enactment: HB 1192 (2026), “Seed laws,” incorporating administrative rules into Indiana Seed Law, passed, signed by the Governor 4 March 2026, Public Law 82, effective 1 July 2026 (LegiScan bill page; enrolled text). It amends IC 15-15-1-34 and adds IC 15-15-1-34.5, effective 1 July 2026, under which a distributor of agricultural or vegetable seed may apply for a permit to use its own labelling, report quantity sold, and pay the inspection fee on that report, with inspection fees of $0.30 per 100 pounds for alfalfa/clover/vegetable/grass seed and mixtures (minimum $0.075 per package/container over 1 lb) and $0.15 per 100 pounds for all other agricultural seed (minimum $0.0375 per package/container over 1 lb), subject to the section’s other subsections. HB 1192 enrolled text, accessed 28–29 Oct 2026.
  • Consequence: Do not present the $45/brand figure as definitively current after 1 July 2026 without re-reading current IC 15-15-1-34 as amended by P.L.82. The hemp-specific rule is unaffected: IC 15-15-13-9(b) still requires a seller of agricultural hemp seed to a grower to hold a permit under IC 15-15-1-34 (section number as cited in IC 15-15-13-9; whether P.L.82 renumbered the operative permit to §34.5 for reporting-permit holders is Verify).

7.5 Licence term, renewal, application processing

IC 15-15-13-8(c) (FindLaw, current as of 1 Jan 2026; added P.L.165-2014, amended P.L.190-2019, §9): “A hemp license or agricultural hemp seed production license expires on December 31 of the year for which the license was issued, unless revoked. A hemp license or agricultural hemp seed production license may be renewed in accordance with rules adopted by the state seed commissioner and is nontransferable.” FindLaw IC 15-15-13-8, accessed 28–29 Oct 2026.

  • Processing (IC 15-15-13-8(a)–(b)): On receipt, the Commissioner either forwards the application to the Indiana State Police — which performs a state or national criminal-history check, determines whether the §7(c)(5) prior-conviction requirement is met, and returns the application with its determinations and a copy of the check — or performs the check itself under the same standards and makes the same determination. The Commissioner then reviews the application and check and, if all chapter requirements are met and a licence should be granted, approves issuance.
  • Plan renewal/application window (Plan §§39–40, via extracts): §39 — a licence application for any given year “may be initiated when publicly announced by the state seed commissioner, but no earlier than October 1 of the year preceding the year for which the license is sought.” §40 — “An applicant for renewal of a hemp license … is subject to any and all requirements of an applicant for a license” — i.e. renewal is not a light-touch rollover; a fresh background-check cycle is within the rule’s wording, though the current Mi-Co renewal workflow itself was not directly inspected in this run (Verify).
  • Unique licence numbers: Plan SOP 0308-HP Rev. 02: “Each grower, handler or facility is given a unique license number that remains constant over the years.” Changes are captured via a licensee portal (username/password per designated representative) or by an OISC administrator.
  • Revocation / refusal: IC 15-15-13-13(a) — Commissioner may revoke or refuse to issue/renew a hemp or agricultural-hemp-seed-production licence and impose a civil penalty (≤$2,500) for violation of a licence requirement, licence terms/conditions, or a growing/handling rule; §13(c) also allows refusal/revocation for violation of any Commissioner rule pertaining to agricultural operations other than hemp growing/handling. Plan §41 adds revocation for failure to cooperate with the Commissioner, State Police, federal or local law enforcement in inspection/sampling.

7.6 Application fields — statute minimum, plus plan/SOP additions

Statute minimum (IC 15-15-13-7(c)(1)–(9), LawServer direct read 29 Oct 2026; added P.L.165-2014, amended P.L.190-2019, §8): (1) Name and address of the applicant. (2) Name and address of the applicant’s hemp operation. (3) GPS coordinates and legal description of the property used for the hemp operation. (4) If a grower, acreage size of the field where hemp will be grown. (5) Statement signed under penalty of perjury that the applicant has not been convicted of a drug-related felony or misdemeanour in the previous 10 years. (6) Written consent allowing the State Police to conduct a state or national criminal-history background check. (7) Written consent allowing the State Police, the Commissioner, or the Commissioner’s authorised representative, if a licence is issued, to conduct aerial inspections and enter the premises to conduct physical inspections and ensure plants meet the §6 hemp definition. (8) Nonrefundable application fee (including background-check cost), in an amount determined by the Commissioner. (9) Any other information required by the Commissioner. LawServer IC 15-15-13-7.

Plan / SOP additions (USDA Plan; SOP 0308-HP Rev. 02; Plan §§33, 37–38 — via extracts, so plan-level, not statute): Age: applicant must be not less than 18 years of age when first applying (plan, via search extract — Verify, as IC 15-15-13 itself does not state 18 in the excerpts located). Key participants: names and titles of key participants; business full name and principal location; EIN if applicable; applicant/operation email(s) and telephone(s) (SOP §2(b)). Third-party sampling/laboratory results, including the third-party entity’s name, address, and contact information (SOP §2(b)). Background check: FBI Identity History Summary, applied for not more than 60 days before submitting the application, paid by the applicant; 10-year lookback for drug-related felony/misdemeanour; a person with a state or federal felony relating to a controlled substance is ineligible for 10 years from conviction, except a person lawfully growing hemp under the 2014 Farm Bill before 20 Dec 2018 whose conviction predates that date (plan-level; Verify against current application instructions). Crop plan: crop-monitoring/testing plan; after 31 Dec 2022, declaration/attestation of a DEA-registered laboratory for total-THC testing, with regular grower/receiving-handler monitoring during growth and a plan to take down a crop trending over the acceptable THC level; annual harvest and disposal report on the Commissioner’s official form. Site detail (Purdue Extension / Krieg DeVault application summaries, 2021): every growing, drying, and processing site; interactive map for latitude/longitude; indoor vs. outdoor designation; facility size; seed variety and growing purpose (seed breeding, seed oil, fibre, grain, cannabinoid, etc.); attestation of having read the “THC Results by Variety” document; attestation that crops will be processed by the applicant personally or sold under a buyer’s agreement / letter of intent. FSA registration (2021 guidance): growers must register the hemp site with the local Farm Service Agency office and report the crop after planting; FSA field/subfield numbers to be entered into Mi-Co by 15 July for each variety on each plot — historical guidance, current FSA deadline/process Verify.

Minimum growing space / plant counts (Plan minimum-size provisions, via extract — plan rule, not statute; section number not asserted — extracts differ): production field (seed sown) 10 contiguous acres minimum (grain/seed/fibre/seed oil); production field (clones/cuttings) 1 acre contiguous minimum, minimum 1,500 plants as a single plot (CBD and other cannabinoids, feminised seed); agricultural hemp seed breeding farm not to exceed 5 acres (AOSCA standards); minimum indoor greenhouse 2,000 sq ft, minimum 1,500 plants (clone production) or 300 mature plants (cannabinoid production); hoop/slat house for starts only — same 2,000 sq ft / 1,500 / 300 figures; indoor (including rotational compactor or similar) — same; mother plants for propagation/cloning may be overwintered, not to exceed 100 total plants, not allowed in a residence. Growing/possessing below the minimum is a violation unless it is university-employed researcher / licensed breeder activity. Whether these minima are still enforced in the 2026 season is Verify — stated here as the Plan text retrieved, not as a confirmed 2026 OISC instruction.

Confidentiality — verbatim statute (IC 15-15-13-17; added P.L.134-2015, §1; amended P.L.190-2019, §18): “Sec. 17. (a) The seed commissioner may keep the: (1) names of growers and handlers who are licensed under this chapter; and (2) locations of licensed hemp crops; confidential for purposes of IC 5-14-3. (b) The seed commissioner may share confidential information under subsection (a) with the state police department, and law enforcement officers (as defined in IC 35-31.5-2-185), and federal enforcement officers (as defined in IC 35-31.5-2-129).” Note the word “may”: confidentiality is discretionary, not mandatory — consistent with OISC publishing a directory-opt-in contact list while withholding the full licence record and crop locations. The practical consequence is the same either way: there is no complete, public, downloadable Indiana hemp licence-holder list, and none should be implied from the partial directory lists above.

Official counts carried forward from Run 1 (unchanged): 2024 — 54 licences (24 grower, 8 grower-handler, 22 handler) in 41 counties (OISC 2024 Hemp Report); FY 2025 — 60 licences (25 grower, 25 handler, 10 grower-handler) (LSA SB 250 notes); 2025 season — 21 licensed growers, 165 acres cannabinoid + 222 acres grain, 0 acres fibre (Purdue Extension, May 2025, citing USDA National Hemp Report 17 Apr 2025). No separate agricultural-hemp-seed-production, researcher, or breeder count was located. Reconciliation (audit, 30 Oct 2026): the 54 and 60 figures are different years, and the 21-grower figure is a 2025-season grower count, not a licence total — they are not competing totals for the same thing.

PART 8: CANNABIS-ZONED PROPERTIES

8.1 Headline: ZERO qualifying listings found

No publicly listed property in Indiana was found that is (a) located in Indiana, (b) marketed for hemp cultivation, hemp processing, or hemp-product retail, and (c) evidenced by an address plus zoning or licence-related information in the listing itself. Every Indiana-city page on the specialist marketplace returns either no listings or only nearby out-of-state listings, and general commercial platforms returned no Indiana hemp-marketed property with address + zoning.

Why “hemp-zoned” barely exists as a category in Indiana (method note): Indiana has no marijuana licensing and therefore no cannabis overlay zoning; hemp cultivation is an agricultural activity licensed site-by-site by OISC (GPS + legal description, Part 7 §6), and IC 15-15-13-21 preempts counties/cities/towns/townships from adopting or enforcing ordinances restricting or regulating hemp growth, production, or processing or any subject regulated by the chapter (Run 1). So a qualifying Indiana listing would in practice be agricultural or industrial land/buildings marketed for hemp use — not a parcel carrying a cannabis zoning code. None meeting that test was found.

Search limitation (stated plainly): Crexi and LoopNet do not expose a reliable “hemp/cannabis” keyword filter for Indiana, and 420Property’s Indiana city pages are proximity pages that pull in Michigan/Illinois listings. The zero count is therefore a search-based zero across the named platforms on 28–29 Oct 2026, not a certified search of every MLS entry in Indiana. A live-broker / MLS check would be needed to upgrade it to an exhaustive negative — see research notes V8-01.

9.3 Ballot history and public opinion

No statewide citizen initiative — confirmed. Indiana Constitution, art. 4, § 1 (verbatim, operative clause): “no law shall be enacted, except by bill.” The legislative authority is vested in the General Assembly (Senate + House). Article 16 provides the only amendment route: a legislatively referred constitutional amendment, agreed by a majority of each chamber in two successive sessions with a general election between them, then approved by a majority of voters at a general election. There is no initiated statute and no initiated constitutional amendment. Indiana’s own referendum guidance states the same point in plain terms: “The Indiana Constitution requires that all laws be passed by the state legislature… The State Constitution would have to be amended before the voters could pass any initiative” — and it distinguishes advisory referenda (officials need not comply) from mandatory referenda (officials must comply), with each referendum’s own authorising law deciding which it is. Indiana Election Division referendum brochure; Ballotpedia, Indiana 2026 ballot measures (“State law does not allow for initiatives or referendums; therefore, all measures are referred to the ballot by the Indiana State Legislature”); Ballotpedia, States with initiative or referendum (Indiana listed among states with no statewide citizen-initiated measures), accessed 28–29 Oct 2026.

Proposed, not enacted: SJR 0024 (2025) would have proposed an art. 4 amendment reserving initiative/referendum power to the people (5% signature threshold in the introduced text) — introduced text only, Verify final status on iga; it did not change the Constitution. A 2026 joint resolution on the same subject (SJR 0011, per BillTrack50, introduced 8 Dec 2025, referred to Judiciary) is likewise PROPOSED only — Verify on iga. Neither creates a usable initiative route today.

Statewide cannabis ballot questions: none located, ever. No statewide public question on medical marijuana, adult-use marijuana, decriminalisation or hemp has been put to Indiana voters, because no such legislatively referred amendment has been passed in two sessions. The only statewide questions certified for 3 Nov 2026 (per Ballotpedia, 28 Oct 2026) concern bail and city/town court-judge residency — neither is cannabis-related.

Local / non-binding ballot questions: none located for Indiana — with a scope limit. Searches for Indiana local advisory cannabis questions (2024–2025, county election results, city-council resolutions) returned out-of-state results (Colorado, Illinois, Ohio, Wisconsin) and no Indiana cannabis question. This is a negative search result, not an exhaustive 92-county canvass: the Election Division’s past-results portal is county-administered, and no Indiana local cannabis question was found in the sources checked. Mark as Unavailable for a definitive “never” claim; a county-by-county check of clerk results would be needed to close it. Local governments can place advisory questions where a statute authorises a referendum, but no Indiana cannabis example was verified.

Public opinion polling — secondary only, no polls invented.
