Polling takeaway: support for at least medical legalisation has been a majority in every Hoosier Survey wave checked (2023: ~86% any-legalisation on the 54.2+32.2 split; 2025: 84%), while the legislature has enacted no medical or adult-use law and in 2025 enacted an advertising ban instead. That gap is a political fact, not a legal change.

PART 10: TRIBAL AND INTERSTATE

10.1 Tribes with land in Indiana

Only one federally recognised tribe is listed for Indiana: the Pokagon Band of Potawatomi Indians, Michigan and Indiana. A second tribe, the Miami Tribe of Oklahoma, also holds trust land / an office in Indiana (below). No other federally recognised tribe with Indiana land was located, and Indiana has no state-recognised tribe with a cannabis/hemp programme located.

Pokagon Band — land in Indiana (official sources)

  • Federal recognition restored by the Pokagon Restoration Act, Pub. L. 103-323 (21 Sept 1994). The Act’s service area (§ 7) is: Michigan counties Allegan, Berrien, Van Buren, Cass and Indiana counties La Porte, St. Joseph, Elkhart, Starke, Marshall, and Kosciusko. Service area ≠ trust land and ≠ jurisdiction over all land in those counties — do not conflate them. Congress.gov, S.1066 text, §§ 6–8.
  • Trust land in Indiana: ~165.81 acres in the City of South Bend, St. Joseph County, taken into trust by BIA decision 17 Nov 2016 (Federal Register notice 12 Dec 2016). The parcel is described in secondary reporting as between Prairie Avenue, U.S. 31 and Locust Road, southwest of downtown South Bend; the Federal Register legal description gives the St. Joseph County parcel by metes and bounds — a street address for the whole parcel was not verified from the Federal Register text in this run; the casino address 3000 Prairie Road, South Bend, is a business address from Four Winds material, not a trust-parcel address — Verify before using it as the parcel address. Four Winds South Bend opened on that trust land in 2018. Federal Register, 12 Dec 2016 trust decision; Michigan CRA release, 18 Dec 2025.
  • No other Pokagon trust parcel in Indiana was located. In particular, no Pokagon trust land in Elkhart County (the alternative casino site considered in the 2016 FEIS) was taken into trust on the sources checked — Verify against the current BIA trust-land record if a parcel list is required.

Miami Tribe of Oklahoma — land in Indiana (official state source, limited detail)

  • The Indiana Department of Natural Resources (Historic Preservation & Archaeology) states the Miami Tribe of Oklahoma “have trust land and a Cultural Resource Extension Office in Fort Wayne.” The INAIAC-sourced state summary states: “there are two tribes that have land in Indiana” — Pokagon and Miami Tribe of Oklahoma — and that the Miami office serves tribal members living in Indiana for gatherings, ceremonies and education. IN DNR Native Americans page; in.gov Native Americans in Indiana page.
  • Parcel size, address and legal description for the Miami Fort Wayne land were NOT located — Unavailable. Do not state an acreage or address.

Miami Nation of Indiana — not federally recognised, no land-based programme

The Miami Nation of Indians of Indiana is an unrecognised group that unsuccessfully sought separate federal recognition; its own history page notes it was “the only tribe to be recognized in the State of Indiana post-removal” in a treaty-land sense, which is not federal recognition and must not be presented as such. A 2011 state-recognition bill (SB 311) did not advance to a vote (secondary) — Verify on iga. No Miami Nation of Indiana cannabis/hemp ordinance or operation was located. Miami Indians history page.

Pokagon cannabis — Michigan only; NOTHING in Indiana

  • The Band legalised adult-use cannabis on its tribal land in April 2023 and launched Rolling Embers retail store + consumption lounge at 19279 Kinst Road, New Buffalo, Michigan — on Michigan tribal land, for customers 21+ with valid ID from any state (secondary business report). Products were sourced from approved Michigan cultivators/manufacturers.
  • 18 Dec 2025: Pokagon–Michigan tribal-state cannabis compact (Michigan’s second, after Bay Mills). The Michigan Cannabis Regulatory Agency release states the compact grants access to Michigan’s licensed adult-use market: purchase from / sell to state licensees; transport cannabis outside Indian lands for transactions with state licensees; integrate into Michigan’s Metrc seed-to-sale system; collaborate on inspections/enforcement; share a percentage of Cannabis Excise Tax with Michigan to reimburse compact administration, remainder to tribal government programmes. Michigan CRA release, 18 Dec 2025.
  • The Band’s own Cannabis Regulatory Act / Cannabis Regulations (Cannabis Regulatory Commission) exist as tribal law — the regulations PDF covers facility and supplier licences, tracking, employees, operations, testing, labelling, packaging, marketing, designated consumption areas and hearings (Pokagon Cannabis Regulations PDF). Scope limit found in this run: the extracted text does not name South Bend or Indiana, and no provision was located extending the tribal cannabis regime to the South Bend trust land. Do not present the tribal cannabis regulations as applying in Indiana.
  • No Pokagon cannabis dispensary, cultivation site, hemp operation or cannabis ordinance on Indiana trust land was located. No Pokagon hemp operation (Indiana or Michigan) was located. No USDA tribal hemp plan for the Pokagon Band was located — the USDA approved-plans list must be checked directly (USDA approved-plans list) and the absence is recorded as Unavailable, not as proof no plan was ever submitted.
  • No Indiana–Pokagon cannabis compact exists. The Pokagon–Indiana compact on BIA’s site (2 July 2021) is a gaming compact, not cannabis: BIA gaming compact page — do not cite it as a cannabis agreement.

Legal framework (official sources only for the legal points; general principles flagged where the source is not Indiana-specific)

  • Tribal sovereignty: the Pokagon Restoration Act provides the Band “shall have jurisdiction to the full extent allowed by law over all lands taken into trust” (§ 8) and that trust land “shall become part of the Band’s reservation” (§ 6). The Band’s own regulations state state-law provisions included in them are adopted “solely as a matter of Band law” and that nothing in them grants/expands/confirms any state jurisdiction.
  • Federal overlay (flagged, not Indiana-verified): marijuana remains federally controlled; a tribal cannabis regime on Indian lands does not, by itself, make transport off those lands lawful, and does not override the destination state’s law. Indiana’s PL 280 / state-criminal-jurisdiction status over Pokagon trust land was not verified from an official source in this run — Verify; do not assert a PL 280 conclusion.
  • Practical consequence: an Indiana resident who lawfully buys at Rolling Embers in Michigan is lawful in Michigan (and, since Dec 2025, within the Michigan compact system). The moment the product enters Indiana, Indiana possession law applies (see §10.2). The South Bend trust land does not create a lawful Indiana cannabis outlet on current sources.

10.2 Interstate

Hemp shipments — Indiana law

  • IC 15-15-13-11 (as added P.L.165-2014, amended P.L.190-2019, § 12): only a hemp licensee, the licensee’s designee or agent may transport hemp off a production site, and the transporter must carry the State Seed Commissioner’s licensing documents evidencing the hemp is from certified seed produced by a licensed grower. The statute as extracted requires licensing documents, not a certificate of analysis — do not add a COA-carry requirement that is not in § 11 (carrying test results is prudent practice, not the § 11 text). OISC statute PDF.
  • Finished-product shipment: no Indiana finished-product shipping licence exists (Run 1, Part 4). Low-THC hemp extract that meets IC 24-4-21 (COA, labelling) may be distributed/sold in Indiana; extract that does not meet it is not protected by the hemp framework.
  • Smokable hemp: the only transit protection in current Indiana law is the continuous-transit exception in IC 35-48-4-10.1(c) (SEA 335, P.L.142-2020, § 81): licensed producer in another state → licensed handler in any state, in continuous transit. Personal or retail import of smokable hemp for Indiana sale/use is not covered by that exception.
  • Federal hemp-transport protection (7 U.S.C. § 1639o — no state may prohibit transport/shipment of hemp produced in compliance with the USDA programme) was reported secondarily and is Verify against the official U.S. Code text before quoting it as a dossier legal conclusion. It does not, in any event, protect marijuana or non-compliant hemp, and does not stop a roadside stop where hemp and marijuana are indistinguishable by sight/smell.

Indiana residents buying in neighbouring states — what is lawful where, and what happens on return

NeighbourLawful purchase there (21+, valid ID) — NOT Indiana lawWhat happens when brought into Indiana
MichiganAdult-use legal since 2018 (Proposal 1); visitors 21+ may buy at licensed retailers on the same per-transaction limits as residents (2.5 oz flower / 15 g concentrate per transaction; public possession 2.5 oz, home possession up to 10 oz with >2.5 oz locked — secondary summaries). Tribal Rolling Embers (New Buffalo) is minutes from the Indiana line and is a documented cross-border draw.Illegal in Indiana on entry. Possession is IC 35-48-4-11 (Class B base, etc., Run 1) regardless of a Michigan receipt, packaging or licence. Michigan permission is expressly territorial: Michigan law covers possession/purchase/transport inside Michigan; crossing the line is a different jurisdiction and a federal interstate-transport exposure. Official MCL text Verify for Run 2.
IllinoisAdult-use legal (Cannabis Regulation and Tax Act, 2019). 2026 changes: SB 3222 / Public Act 104-0463, signed 12 June 2026: under-21 sales of intoxicating hemp banned immediately; resident possession doubled to 60 g flower / 1,000 mg infused THC / 10 g concentrate (non-residents half); from 12 Nov 2026 intoxicating hemp products over 0.4 mg total THC per container are treated as cannabis and sold only through licensed dispensaries, and the old Industrial Hemp Act is replaced by a new Illinois Hemp Act. Illinois does not wait for the federal 11 Dec 2026 date.Same Indiana result: lawful Illinois purchase ≠ lawful Indiana possession. Illinois’s 12 Nov 2026 dispensary-only rule also narrows what an Indiana resident can even buy at general retail in Illinois after that date — but that is Illinois law, and it does not create any Indiana permission. Official Illinois General Assembly text Verify.
OhioAdult-use legal for 21+; SB 56 (signed 19 Dec 2025, eff. 20 Mar 2026) repealed voter-enacted ch. 3780 and consolidated medical + adult-use into rewritten ORC ch. 3796. Possession 2.5 oz plant + 15 g extract cumulative; home grow 6/adult, 12/residence. Critical for Indiana residents: under SB 56, lawful possession is tied to Ohio-licensed dispensary or lawful Ohio home grow — marijuana bought in another state is not protected in Ohio either.Same Indiana result on return to Indiana. Ohio law is included here only to prevent the error of treating “legal in Ohio” as portable. ORC text Verify.
Kentucky (south)Medical only, programme launching 2025; no adult-use. Included only to complete the border picture — no Kentucky purchase creates an Indiana right.Same Indiana result.

Border enforcement — what is actually evidenced (no official count located)

  • ISP position (secondary press, 2019–2020): ISP Superintendent Doug Carter warned residents buying in Michigan/Illinois “Don’t. Get. Caught” and said “That substance in Indiana is still illegal,” while also saying ISP had not seen the predicted uptick in marijuana violations and had not increased border patrols or specifically targeted personal-quantity dispensary trips. ISP spokesman (2019, on Illinois legalisation): “Regardless of the legislation passed by our neighboring states, we will uphold the criminal laws of Indiana… We do not recognize any cards or documents from other states.” Merry Jane / WTHR interview; IndyPolitics, accessed 28–29 Oct 2026.
  • Individual arrest examples are secondary only and are not a trend: a Chicago man arrested in Indiana after a Michigan purchase of 241 g (Times of Northwest Indiana, via MJBizDaily); a La Porte County I-94 stop (year as reported) with ~12 one-pound packages plus cartridges/edibles, charged as Level 5 dealing (secondary crime report). No official ISP or court aggregate for border-area marijuana arrests was located — Unavailable. Do not present these cases as an enforcement rate.
  • No Indiana interstate cannabis/hemp compact, agreement or memorandum was located — not with Michigan, Illinois, Ohio or Kentucky, and not a tribal cannabis compact with Indiana. The only Indiana–Pokagon compact located is the 2021 gaming compact (§10.1). The Michigan–Pokagon cannabis compact (Dec 2025) is a Michigan agreement and does not bind Indiana.

What CAN legally be brought into Indiana (summary, subject to the Verify flags above)

  1. Compliant hemp (licensed, ≤0.3% delta-9, with § 11 licensing documents if moved off a production site by a licensee/designee/agent).
  2. Low-THC hemp extract meeting IC 24-4-21 (COA, QR/barcode labelling) — including extract lawfully bought elsewhere if it meets Indiana’s definition and labelling; out-of-state origin alone does not disqualify extract, but non-compliant or mislabelled product is not protected.
  3. Smokable hemp only in continuous licensed-producer → licensed-handler transit.
  4. Nothing else marijuana-derived: no marijuana flower, edibles, concentrates, or intoxicating-hemp products that fall under AG Opinion 2023-1’s Schedule I reading can be brought in on the basis of a neighbouring state’s licence, a medical card from any state, or a tribal purchase in Michigan. Indiana recognises no out-of-state medical card (Run 1).

PART 11: OPERATIONS RULES

The split that drives almost every answer in Part 11 is this: Indiana licenses hemp growers and handlers under IC 15-15-13. It does not license finished-product manufacturers, distributors, retailers, or carriers, and it has no cannabis licence of any kind. A “hemp product business” that only buys, makes, or sells finished low-THC hemp extract therefore has no hemp licence to transfer, renew, relocate, or revoke — its duties are the general business, sales-tax, food, and deceptive-sales duties plus the product-specific duties in IC 24-4-21. Where a product business also grows or handles raw hemp, the grower/handler rules apply to that activity. Method note: a direct open of the USDA Indiana Hemp Plan PDF failed in the Part 11 research session on a transient tool error and was not retried. Plan provisions below are therefore cited from the indexed text of that same PDF, which Run 1 had already used, and are marked Verify for a direct re-open before publication. Statute text below is quoted from Justia/FindLaw mirrors of the Indiana Code; the brief’s preference for iga.in.gov direct text could not be satisfied for every section in this run, so those quotations are also marked Verify against iga.in.gov before publication.

11.1 Testing

(a) Growers — pre-harvest / regulatory testing (the only mandatory hemp testing regime in Indiana)

ItemRuleSource
What is testedTotal THC on a dry-weight basis. The OISC plan uses gas chromatography or liquid chromatography under OISC methods MTD 0500-FD (GC) and MTD 0502-FD (LC). Total THC combines THC and THCA.USDA Indiana Hemp Plan (indexed text), §§65–66; OISC FAQ 10 Oct 2020: “Total THC combines THC and THC-A” — Indiana Hemp Plan PDF; OISC FAQ PDF (via CBD Seed Co mirror)
Pass standard≤0.3% total THC, applying measurement uncertainty — i.e. a sample passes if the range produced by applying the margin of error includes 0.3% or lower.USDA Indiana Hemp Plan (indexed text)
Sampling protocolField sampling under OISC MTD 0501-IS, “Field Sampling of Hemp”: standard and performance-based sampling sufficient at 95% confidence that no more than 1% of plants in each lot exceed the acceptable level, collecting a representative, homogeneous composite sample. Secondary summaries describe 5–8-inch cuttings from the top of flower material, each variety sampled separately into one composite. Verify the cutting-length detail against MTD 0501-IS itself — it was not located as a standalone document in this run.USDA Indiana Hemp Plan, §65
Who samplesOfficial pre-harvest sampling is conducted by the State Seed Commissioner’s authorised representative; an authorised representative of the grower must be present. The grower must harvest within 30 days of sampling or a second pre-harvest sampling is required.USDA Indiana Hemp Plan (indexed text); secondary summary consistent — Verify the 30-day harvest window in the current plan text
Surveillance / random testingIC 15-15-13-12 charges the Seed Commissioner with monitoring licensed hemp and conducting random testing for THC compliance. IC 15-15-13-9(d): the Commissioner may inspect any hemp crop during growth and take a representative composite sample; if a crop averages >0.3% delta-9 THC dry weight, the Commissioner may detain, seize, or embargo it.IC 15-15-13-9, IC 15-15-13-12 — IC 15-15-13-9; OISC statute PDF
Applicant’s own testing planUnder the plan, after 31 Dec 2022 an applicant must declare/attest to a DEA-registered laboratory for total-THC testing and present a crop-testing plan including regular monitoring during growth and a plan to take the crop down if it trends over the limit.USDA Indiana Hemp Plan (indexed text, via secondary plan summary) — Verify current wording
No other mandatory panel for growersNo Indiana rule located requiring growers to test for pesticides, heavy metals, microbials, mycotoxins, or residual solvents on the crop. Those panels appear only in proposed bills (e.g. 2026 HB 1130 craft-hemp-flower text, not enacted).—

(b) Hemp product businesses — finished low-THC hemp extract testing

ItemRuleSource
Mandatory testIC 24-4-21-3: a distributor may distribute low-THC hemp extract only if it has a certificate of analysis (COA) prepared by an independent testing laboratory showing (1) the extract is the product of a batch tested by that lab, (2) the batch contained ≤0.3% total delta-9 THC, including precursors, by weight, based on testing of a random sample of the batch, and (3) the cannabidiol percentage present.IC 24-4-21-3 (via Run 1; SEA 516 enrolled text) — SB 516 enrolled text
What is NOT requiredNo Indiana rule located in current IC 24-4-21 requiring a finished-product panel for pesticides, metals, solvents, microbials, or mycotoxins; no state potency-lab accreditation scheme for finished products; no state recall statute for low-THC hemp extract. Violation of IC 24-4-21 is a Class B infraction (Class A if a prior unrelated judgment) under IC 24-4-21-5, plus general deceptive-sales liability under IC 24-5-0.5.IC 24-4-21-3, -5
Food-safety plan (handlers who process into food-type products)The USDA plan states a processor must maintain and have available for inspection by the Indiana Department of Health or OISC a food-safety plan and practice compliant with 21 CFR Part 111, Part 501, or Indiana Department of Health standards as applicable to its own operations. Verify — this is plan text, and its current enforcement posture for finished-product businesses was not confirmed in this run.USDA Indiana Hemp Plan (indexed text)
RecallsNo Indiana hemp-product recall rule, recall database, or recall procedure was located. A failed COA (over-limit total THC) means the batch does not meet IC 24-4-21-3 and may not lawfully be distributed as low-THC hemp extract; it does not trigger a published state recall process. Federal FDA/FTC action for adulterated/misbranded food or unapproved drug claims remains a separate federal exposure (see Part 12, §7).—

(c) Accredited / DEA-registered labs serving Indiana

  • OISC cooperative laboratories: OISC “may license analytical laboratories to conduct analyses of hemp for total THC, other cannabinoids, or constituent compounds.” Participating labs “must be certified by nationally recognized standards, such as current ISO 17025 standards, or similar standards approved by the State Seed Commissioner, must be registered with the [DEA] for schedule 1 controlled drug substances, and comply with all other federal or local laws. All laboratory quality management systems and data are subject to audit by OISC.” The OISC cooperative-labs page lists one approved cooperating laboratory: Agrozen Labs, Lebanon, IN (business public contact details are on the OISC page and are not reproduced here). OISC cooperative labs page.
  • USDA DEA-laboratory directory: USDA maintains the national directory of DEA-registered analytical hemp testing laboratories; Agrozen Laboratory, Lebanon, Indiana, appears on it. OISC warns that “Indiana laboratories represented on this site may not be licensed cooperating hemp laboratories with OISC.” USDA DEA laboratories directory.
  • Historical note (secondary, dated): Agrozen Labs announced 26 Oct 2020 that it was Indiana’s first USDA-certified / DEA Schedule I-certified hemp testing laboratory and the first OISC-approved cooperative lab. Morning Ag Clips; Seed World, 26 Oct 2020.
  • Federal baseline interaction: Per the user-supplied baseline, USDA enforcement of DEA-registered-lab testing for hemp is delayed to 31 Dec 2026. No Indiana source found in this run contradicts that date, and no Indiana state mirror of the 12 Nov / 11 Dec 2026 federal hemp dates was enacted (SB 250 died). Practical consequence for Indiana growers: the plan’s DEA-lab attestation requirement and the federal enforcement delay coexist uneasily — the plan text predates the delay, and whether OISC is currently enforcing DEA-lab-only testing for the 2026 crop is Verify with OISC. Do not present either “any lab will do” or “only DEA labs, enforced today” as settled.
  • Labs for finished products: IC 24-4-21-3 requires only an “independent testing laboratory” — it does not on its face require DEA registration or ISO 17025 for the finished-product COA. Verify whether OISC or the courts read any accreditation requirement into “independent testing laboratory.”

(d) Failed tests and remediation — growers and handlers

  • Statute: IC 15-15-13-9(d) — over-threshold crop may be detained, seized, or embargoed (see §11.1(a)).
  • Plan — growers: A grower aware a crop exceeds the acceptable level, whether notified or not, shall: “(A) harvest the crop within thirty (30) days of sampling; (B) securely maintain the entire crop on the farm or property where the crop was grown; (C) coordinate with the state seed commissioner for the disposal to be witnessed by the Indiana state police or their local law enforcement designee; (D) dispose of the crop in a designated site in a manner that complies with local and state burn laws and this rule; (E) verify disposal of the crop with the state seed commissioner, including photographic or video evidence of disposal.” Disposal must occur on the farm/property where grown. USDA Indiana Hemp Plan (indexed text).
  • Plan — handlers: A handler “must destroy all excess THC in their control whether: (A) as a separated extract obtained through extraction of desired legally held cannabinoids, e.g., CBD, CBN, or other legal cannabinoid; or (B) as unprocessed hemp plant material that cannot be redeemed for other legal use. Only extracts of cannabinoids containing not more than the acceptable hemp THC level are compliant.” Transfers of THC-containing extracts or hemp exceeding 0.3% THC to another person are not allowed. USDA Indiana Hemp Plan (indexed text).
  • Remediation: The plan states “Remediation can be performed in accordance with Section 47 of this rule on crops that do not meet the acceptable hemp THC level.” Section 47’s operative remediation method was not extracted in this run — Verify. The older OISC FAQ (10 Oct 2020) and older licence form describe a different, now-stale picture: fields averaging >1.0% THC destroyed; fields 0.3–1.0% potentially diverted for an approved (fiber) use, with the FAQ itself noting USDA rules forbade the fiber diversion and that the question was unresolved. Do not use the 0.3–1.0% diversion description as current law — it conflicts with the plan’s “must be destroyed / irretrievable and noningestible” language and with federal rules. The federal negligent-violation safe harbour (a producer who made reasonable efforts is not negligent if the crop is below 1.0% total THC, 7 CFR 990.31, as summarised in secondary sources) is a federal negligence concept, not an Indiana permission to sell 0.3–1.0% material.
  • No state “re-test to pass” rule located for growers beyond the plan’s sampling provisions; no published Indiana failed-test register or recall list was located.

11.2 Licence rules — growers/handlers only

TopicGrower / handler / seed-production licenceHemp product business (no hemp licence)
Term“A hemp license or agricultural hemp seed production license expires on December 31 of the year for which the license was issued, unless revoked.” IC 15-15-13-8(c).N/A — no licence to expire.
Renewal“…may be renewed in accordance with rules adopted by the state seed commissioner.” IC 15-15-13-8(c). No renewal deadline, renewal fee schedule, or renewal form was located as a current standalone source in this run — Verify against the current OISC portal. Fees in LSA notes: grower $750, handler $750, both $1,500, site change $50 (Run 1).N/A.
TransferNontransferable. IC 15-15-13-8(c): “…and is nontransferable.” The older licence form repeats: “A license, when granted … is non transferable and is valid for one growing season (1 year) unless revoked. A license is subject to renewal.”N/A. A sale of a product business does not transfer any hemp licence; the buyer must apply anew for any grower/handler activity.
Ownership changeNo standalone ownership-change rule located. Because the licence is nontransferable and key participants must pass background checks (see §11.8), a change in the person(s) controlling the licensed operation should be treated as requiring a new application / OISC approval in practice — but that is inference, not a located rule. Verify with OISC before advising on any entity sale, merger, or key-participant substitution.General business-entity filings (INBiz) only; no hemp-licence ownership filing exists.
Relocation / site changeSites are licence-specific (GPS + legal description, IC 15-15-13-7). The portal allows site changes; LSA notes list a $50 site-change fee. Secondary portal guidance: a grower/handler must report drying/processing-site changes and grow-site changes via OISC forms, and hemp grown/processed outside approved areas is not covered. Verify the current site-change form and whether a site change requires re-inspection.N/A for product-only businesses; a change of retail/manufacturing premises triggers only general business, tax (RRMC per location, $25), and local zoning consequences.
Revocation / refusalIC 15-15-13-13(a): the Seed Commissioner “may revoke or refuse to issue or renew a hemp license or an agricultural hemp seed production license and may impose a civil penalty” for violation of (1) a licence requirement, (2) licence terms/conditions, (3) a rule relating to growing/handling hemp, or (4) IC 15-15-13-19 (bud/flower sale only to licensed processor). Civil penalty cap $2,500 per §13(b); penalties go to ISDA for hemp marketing/research (§13(d)); violator must also reimburse OISC lab-testing costs (§13(e)). IC 15-15-13-13(c): revocation/refusal also available for violation of any Seed Commissioner rule pertaining to agricultural operations other than hemp. IC 15-15-13-9(e): revocation for failure to cooperate with the Commissioner, State Police, or federal/local law enforcement. FindLaw IC 15-15-13-13.N/A — but a product business can lose its RRMC, face IC 24-4-21 infraction judgments, and face criminal exposure for dealing/smokable-hemp offences.
Negligent vs. knowing violationsIC 15-15-13-13.5, verbatim in substance: “(a) Except as provided in subsection (b), the state seed commissioner shall give a person who negligently violates this chapter a reasonable time, determined by the state seed commissioner, to correct the violation without imposing a penalty under section 13 … [and] may require … a corrective action plan … and report … on compliance … (b) A person who commits a negligent violation … three (3) times in a five (5) year period shall immediately be ineligible to produce hemp for five (5) years. (c) If the state seed commissioner believes that a person has knowingly or intentionally violated this chapter, [he] shall notify: (1) the superintendent of the state police department; and (2) the prosecuting attorney of the county in which the violation occurred … (d) A person who commits a negligent violation … is subject to a late fee as established by rule…” Added P.L.190-2019, §15; amended P.L.156-2020, §64. Justia IC 15-15-13-13.5.N/A.
Insurance / bonding minimumsNo OISC insurance or bonding minimum for hemp growers/handlers was located in IC 15-15-13 or the plan. Secondary guidance says crop insurance is “strongly recommended” but “not required by law,” citing USDA Risk Management Agency hemp coverage. Do not present any dollar minimum. Note the adjacent rule that is a minimum: a pesticide business applying pesticides for hire must have liability insurance (OISC pesticide guidance, 12 Mar 2024) — that is a pesticide-business rule, not a hemp-licence rule. OISC pesticide guidance PDF.No hemp-product insurance/bonding minimum located. General business liability insurance is a commercial choice, not a hemp condition. (Proposed SB 250/SB 478 permit systems, which died, would have created permit conditions; they are not law.)

11.3 Cultivation rules

  • Licensing / plot registration: IC 15-15-13-7 application requires, per Run 1: GPS coordinates, legal description of the land, acreage (growers), 10-year drug-conviction perjury statement, background-check consent, inspection consent. The plan adds: records retained by applicant and Commissioner for at least 3 years; each growing, drying, and processing site disclosed; site designated indoor or outdoor; variety and purpose (seed breeding, seed oil, grain, fiber, cannabinoid, etc.) specified. IC 15-15-13-7; USDA Indiana Hemp Plan (indexed text); Krieg DeVault 2021 application summary (secondary).
  • Minimum grow sizes (plan rule; section number not asserted — extracts differ): These are unusually specific and are a compliance trap for small growers: production field, seed-sown (grain/seed/fiber/seed oil): 10 contiguous acres minimum. Production field, clones/cuttings (CBD and other cannabinoids, feminized seed): 1 acre contiguous minimum, minimum 1,500 plants grown as a single plot. Agricultural hemp seed breeding farm: not to exceed 5 acres (AOSCA standards). Minimum indoor grow greenhouse: 2,000 sq ft, minimum 1,500 plants (clones) or 300 mature plants (cannabinoid production). Hoop house / slat house for starts only: 2,000 sq ft, 1,500 plants (clones) or 300 mature plants. Indoor: 2,000 sq ft, 1,500 plants (clones) or 300 mature plants (cannabinoid or feminized-seed production). Growing below the minimum is a violation; undersized sites “may be subject to orders of disposal by the state seed commissioner.” Exceptions: hemp research by a university-employed researcher or a licensed hemp breeder. Mother plants for propagation/cloning may be overwintered, not to exceed 100 total plants; mother plants are not allowed in a residence. USDA Indiana Hemp Plan (indexed text). Verify whether these minima remain in the current rule or were relaxed after 2021 — the OISC FAQ (Oct 2020) says OISC would be “more diligent” on insufficient grows in 2021, and no later amendment was located in this run.
  • Indoor vs. outdoor: No separate indoor lighting, ventilation, canopy, or plant-count cap rule was located beyond the minima above and the site-designation requirement. No Indiana rule located on residential growing — but the mother-plant residence ban and the site-registration requirement together mean an unregistered home grow is not a licensed grow; Run 1’s secondary summary (“residential growing is strictly prohibited”) is consistent with that but is secondary — Verify for a direct rule citation.
  • Pre-harvest sampling: See §11.1(a). Harvest within 30 days of sampling (plan).
  • Seed: All growers/handlers must comply with seed rules: IC 15-15-13-9(a)–(b) — seed-production licence for producing seed for sale to licensed growers/handlers; a seller of agricultural hemp seed to a grower must hold a seed-distributor permit under IC 15-15-1-34; seed must comply with Seed Commissioner standards; OISC seed testing covers THC, purity, noxious weeds, and germination (secondary, Purdue Agriculture News, June 2021).
  • Records / audit / inspection: IC 15-15-13-9(c): growers/handlers must keep records per Commissioner rules; Commissioner may audit on at least 3 days’ notice during normal business hours. Buyer receipts: seller name, place of business, licence number, quantity — buyer retains 2 years (IC 15-15-13-18, per Run 1). Verify the current record-retention period for growers (plan says at least 3 years for site records — the 2-year and 3-year figures apply to different records and are not a contradiction).
  • Product businesses: No cultivation rules apply to a business that does not grow. A handler that dries/processes/transports for others needs a handler licence and the transport documents in the plan (handler licence evidence, driver photo ID, invoice/delivery document with recipient, address, telephone, variety, quantity, and a COA or copy showing total THC).

11.4 Security

  • Growers/handlers — general security: No Indiana rule located imposing fencing, cameras, alarms, guards, or access-log requirements on hemp fields, greenhouses, drying sites, or handler premises. The security duties that do exist are narrower: over-threshold crop must be “securely maintained” on the farm where grown pending witnessed disposal (plan, §11.1(d)); non-compliant material must not be moved off the grower’s/handler’s property, sold, transported, or processed (plan); transport documents and licence evidence must be in the transporter’s immediate possession (plan); licence-form conditions (older form): seed/plants kept in a “non-secured location” is a violation; theft/enhancement/disturbance of a plot must be reported to State Police and the Seed Commissioner. Older form: IH-1 application (2015-era) — older form, Verify current form.
  • OISC laboratory security (not a grower rule): The plan’s lab SOP requires locked storage under the Quality Assurance Director / Feed Chromatography Laboratory Supervisor, a locked laboratory, samples above 0.3% THC in a combination-locked freezer or key-locked safe, and combination changes on employee termination. This is OISC’s own lab, not a grower/handler premises standard — do not apply it to farms.
  • Pesticide storage security (adjacent, not hemp-specific): 355 IAC 5-2-7 / 5-9-2 require pesticide storage to be protected from wildlife, vandalism, and unauthorised access by fencing, lighting, or other approved means, within lockable walls/fences/doors, posted “Danger, Pesticide Storage, Authorized Personnel Only.” OISC pesticide rulemaking docs: 355 IAC 5 revisions.
  • Product businesses: No hemp-specific security rule located (no locked-display, safe, camera, or alarm rule for CBD/hemp-extract retail). General burglary/theft law and any local zoning conditions apply.

11.5 Waste disposal, including non-compliant hemp destruction

  • Non-compliant hemp: See §11.1(d) — on-farm, law-enforcement-witnessed, burn-law-compliant disposal with photographic/video verification; handler excess THC destroyed so as to be “irretrievable and noningestible”; DEA disposal rules govern THC materials regardless of form (plan).
  • Compliant hemp waste (stalks, spent biomass, extraction residue within limit): No Indiana hemp-specific waste rule located — no composting, landfill, incineration, or rendering rule for compliant waste, and no hemp-waste manifest system. General waste law (IC 13-30, IDEM rules) and any local burn ordinances apply; extraction solvents are governed by their own hazardous-waste rules, not by hemp law. Verify with IDEM before advising on solvent-waste disposal.
  • Finished-product waste / unsold product: No Indiana rule located for disposal of expired or unsold low-THC hemp extract. It may not be “remediated” into compliance by dilution — the plan’s transfer ban and the batch-COA requirement mean an over-limit batch cannot lawfully be sold as low-THC hemp extract.
  • Seed waste / noxious weeds: Seed rules (purity, noxious-weed testing) apply at sale; no separate seed-destruction rule was located.

11.6 Environmental: water, pesticides, energy

  • Water: No hemp-specific water-use, irrigation-permit, runoff, or discharge rule located. Hemp is treated as an agricultural crop for general water law; no Indiana hemp water-allocation or water-quality rule was found. Do not import another state’s hemp water rules.
  • Pesticides — this is the one environmental area with a real Indiana regime, and it is a pesticide regime, not a hemp regime: All pesticide products produced/distributed/sold/offered in Indiana must be state-registered (IC 15-16-4-61, cited in OISC guidance). OISC guidance, “Legal Use of Pesticides in Hemp Production in Indiana” (updated 21 Apr 2022): EPA has accepted legal use in hemp on a few FIFRA §3 products; growers may also use §24(c) Special Local Need registrations (copy on hand at application) and §25(b) minimum-risk products (state-registered, ingredients exempt from tolerance, labelled for other agricultural crops, not labelled for residential use). “The label is the law”: for §3 products, “hemp” must be on the EPA-accepted label at application; for §24(c), the grower must have the 24(c) label on hand. OISC states reference to a product “does not indicate acceptance or approval … as safe or effective.” OISC pesticide-in-hemp guidance, 21 Apr 2022; product list: OISC hemp product list. No Indiana hemp-specific pesticide residue limit, pre-harvest interval for hemp, or hemp pesticide-testing panel was located. A secondary list of “approved” products circulates on non-official sites — do not rely on it; the OISC list above is the official route, and its currency (2022 document) is Verify. Pesticide applicator licensing: a business applying pesticides for hire must hold a pesticide business licence, carry liability insurance, and employ a licensed commercial applicator (OISC guidance, 12 Mar 2024). Private applicators have separate certification. This applies to hemp growers only insofar as they apply pesticides.
  • Energy: No Indiana hemp energy rule located — no indoor-grow energy-efficiency, lighting, or reporting standard for hemp. General utility/building codes apply.

11.7 Sampling, giveaways, discounts, promotions

  • Regulatory sampling (growers): See §11.1(a) — “sampling” in Indiana hemp law means OISC/commissioner sampling for THC compliance, not consumer sampling.
  • Consumer sampling / giveaways / discounts / promotions for hemp products: No Indiana rule located — no hemp-specific ban, age-gating, quantity limit, or labelling rule for free samples, BOGO offers, discounts, or promotions of low-THC hemp extract or CBD. The constraints that do apply are general: IC 24-5-0.5 (deceptive consumer sales — a violation of the hemp-derived-cannabinoid chapter was proposed to be a deceptive act in SB 250, which died, so that specific deeming provision is not law); FDA/FTC rules on disease claims; and, for any product that is in fact a Schedule I substance under the AG’s reading (delta-8 etc.), the criminal dealing provisions, not a promotions code.
  • Tobacco/vaping promotions are a different regime and do not transfer to hemp extract: tobacco advertising/sale rules (IC 35-46-1, ATC) apply to tobacco/vaping products, not to CBD.
  • Giveaways to minors: No hemp-specific rule, but giving a Schedule I substance to a minor would be dealing (see Part 12, §2); giving low-THC hemp extract to a minor is not age-restricted by IC 24-4-21 (see Part 12, §2) — that absence is itself the finding, and it should be flagged as a policy gap, not filled by analogy to tobacco.

11.8 Drug testing and background checks

  • Licence holders / key participants (growers/handlers): IC 15-15-13-7(c)(5) + IC 15-15-13-8: application forwarded to State Police (or Commissioner performs equivalent check); state or national criminal-history background check; determination whether the 10-year prior-conviction requirement is met. Per Run 1: signed statement under penalty of perjury of no drug-related felony or misdemeanour conviction in the previous 10 years. Plan/secondary detail: FBI Identity History Summary background check within 60 days of application; a person with a state/federal felony relating to a controlled substance is ineligible for 10 years from conviction, with an exception for a person lawfully growing hemp under the 2014 Farm Bill before 20 Dec 2018 whose conviction predates that date. Verify the 2014-Farm-Bill exception in current plan text. Minimum applicant age in the plan: 18 years (plan summary). Verify — IC 15-15-13 itself does not state 18 in the excerpts located. No ongoing / random drug-testing rule for licence holders was located. The background check is at application (and, by renewal, presumably at renewal — Verify whether renewal requires a fresh FBI check).
  • Staff / employees of licensed operations: No Indiana rule located requiring background checks or drug tests for non-key-participant employees of a hemp grower/handler. The plan’s “key participants” concept (owner / person responsible for the crop) is the limit of the background-check rule as found.
  • Staff of product businesses: No hemp-specific background-check or drug-test rule located (there is no licence to condition). General employment law applies — see Part 12, §5 for the absence of employee protections.
  • Effect of expungement on the 10-year bar: Unavailable / Verify — no Indiana authority located stating whether an expunged drug conviction counts toward IC 15-15-13-7’s 10-year bar. Do not advise that expungement clears the bar, or that it does not, without a source. This is a Run 2 follow-up for OISC/AG guidance.

11.9 Research and university work

  • Origins: Indiana’s programme began as a research programme under the 2014 Farm Bill. The USDA plan and secondary summaries describe it as led by Purdue University and involving seven other Indiana universities. The names of the seven other institutions were not verified in this run — Verify against the plan’s research section before listing them.
  • Purdue’s structural role today: OISC itself sits at Purdue University (Office of Indiana State Chemist & Seed Commissioner, Purdue University, West Lafayette). The Seed Commissioner administers the programme; Purdue Extension publishes hemp production guidance (e.g. “A Growth Spurt in Hemp Production,” May 2025, citing USDA/Cornell data) and the hemp Extension specialist role. Purdue Extension, May 2025.
  • Research licence category: The plan and the older OISC application form (IH-1) provide for research-purpose applications by institutions of higher education, with the institution named on the form and plants subject to sampling by the Commissioner/State Police. Whether a distinct “researcher licence” still exists as a separate licence type in the current portal, and on what terms, is Verify — Run 1 listed “researcher (per USDA plan)” and this run did not locate a current standalone researcher application.
  • Research exceptions to cultivation minima: University-employed hemp researchers are excepted from the minimum grow sizes in the plan (Part 7 §6 / §11.3). Licensed hemp breeders are likewise excepted.
  • Other Indiana universities (current research): No current, dated list of Indiana universities actively conducting hemp research was located beyond Purdue/OISC/Extension. Do not name Ball State, IU, Notre Dame, etc. as hemp researchers without a source. The Midwest Hemp Council is an industry association, not a university, and ISDA’s Indiana Grown programme is a marketing programme, not a research licence.
  • No Indiana university medical-marijuana research programme exists (no medical programme to research under state licence); any Schedule I research would be federal DEA-licensed, not state-licensed.

PART 12: CONSUMER AND SOCIAL RULES

Audit addition (30 Oct 2026) — possession penalties, summary (IC 35-48-4-11; full regime in Run 1). Knowingly or intentionally possessing marijuana, hash oil, hashish or salvia, growing/cultivating marijuana, or knowingly failing to destroy marijuana growing on one's premises is a Class B misdemeanour as the base offence. It is a Class A misdemeanour if the person has a prior drug conviction, or if the substance is packaged to appear as low-THC hemp extract and the person knew or reasonably should have known it was marijuana, hash oil, hashish or salvia. It is a Level 6 felony if the person has a prior drug conviction and possesses at least 30 grams of marijuana or at least 5 grams of hash oil, hashish or salvia. The base Class B is not limited to under 30 grams — the 30-gram figure matters only for the Level 6 enhancement together with a prior conviction.

12.1 Paraphernalia and smoke/vape shop rules

Paraphernalia — corrected statement of the law (flagged correction to Run 1 shorthand):

OffenceCurrent classification (as found)Source
Possession of paraphernalia, IC 35-48-4-8.3Class C misdemeanour (up to 60 days / $500); Class A misdemeanour if the person has a prior unrelated judgment or conviction under the section (not merely a second offence). Possession means possessing a raw material, instrument, device, or other object the person intends to use for introducing a controlled substance into the body, testing its strength/effectiveness/purity, or enhancing its effect. Exclusions in the statute include rolling papers and drug-testing kits/test strips (secondary summary — Verify in current §8.3 text).IC 35-48-4-8.3; LegalClarity (secondary)
Dealing in paraphernalia, IC 35-48-4-8.5(a) Class A infraction for keeping for sale, offering for sale, delivering, or financing delivery of an item for the listed paraphernalia purposes; (b) knowingly or intentionally violating (a) is a Class A misdemeanour, and a Level 6 felony if the person has a prior unrelated judgment or conviction under the section. Exclusions (§8.5(c)): items marketed for lawful research/teaching/chemical analysis and not for sale; items marketed for or historically/customarily used in connection with tobacco or any other lawful substance; syringe-programme entities. Verbatim (b)–(c) from LawServer mirror.LawServer IC 35-48-4-8.5
Manufacturing paraphernalia, IC 35-48-4-8.1Initially a Class A infraction; knowing/intentional violation with a prior judgment for the same offence becomes a Level 6 felony (secondary summary — Verify current text).Secondary summary only

Practical consequence: A smoke shop selling pipes/bongs is not committing a paraphernalia crime merely by selling an item that could be used with tobacco or another lawful substance — the §8.5(c)(2) tobacco/lawful-substance exclusion is the shop’s ordinary defence, and the State must prove the paraphernalia purpose. Conversely, a shop that sells an item for introducing a controlled substance, or that sells smokable hemp itself, is outside that protection (smokable hemp dealing is separately a Class A misdemeanour under IC 35-48-4-10.1).

Smoke/vape shop rules (hemp-specific): None located. There is no Indiana hemp smoke-shop licence, hemp-retailer permit, hemp-shop zoning category, or hemp-shop fee. What applies to such shops is: general business registration + RRMC ($25/location) for sales tax; if the shop sells tobacco/vaping products: ATC tobacco certificate regime, age 21 (IC 35-46-1-10), and the 1,000-foot school buffer for a tobacco/vaping business (IC 35-46-1-11.2 / -11.4, Class C misdemeanour, grandfathering applies) — Run 1, Part 3; local zoning / use-variance law (e.g. Indianapolis BZA 2025-UV2-009, cited in Run 1, where hemp/CBD/smoking-paraphernalia retail was held “not permitted” in that D-5 location); IC 15-15-13-21 local preemption for growing/processing hemp does not create a right to retail hemp products in any zone — retail is not a chapter-regulated subject in that sense. Verify that reading before advising a retailer.

12.2 Minimum age — three different answers, do not conflate

Low-THC hemp extract / CBD (IC 24-4-21 products)None. No statewide 18+ or 21+ purchase age was located in IC 24-4-21 or elsewhere for hemp extract/CBD. Retailer 21+ policies are voluntary.Run 1, §1.4; confirmed by secondary summaries (LegalClarity, CBD Oracle) — but the absence is the finding, so it rests on the absence of any age provision in the chapter text.
Tobacco / vaping products21. IC 35-46-1-10 (sale to person under 21 prohibited); IC 35-46-1-11.2/-11.4 school buffer. A hemp-extract vape sold as a vaping product could attract the tobacco/vaping regime — Verify how ATC treats a hemp-extract vaping device that contains no nicotine/tobacco.IC 35-46-1-10, -11.2, -11.4 (Run 1)
Hemp-derived THC products (delta-8, delta-10, THCP, THC-O, etc.)No age rule — because there is no lawful retail category. Under AG Opinion 2023-1 these are Schedule I (opinion only, not statute, not binding on courts). If treated as Schedule I marijuana, sale is dealing under IC 35-48-4-10, and sale to a minor is an elevating factor (Level 5 felony for dealing ≥10 lb / ≥300 g or sale to a minor, per Run 1’s §1.1 summary — Verify the current dealing-to-minor elevation in IC 35-48-4-10). Secondary summaries agree: “Indiana currently has no state-mandated minimum age to purchase hemp-derived delta-8 products. Most retailers voluntarily restrict sales to customers 21 and older.”AG Opinion 2023-1; LegalClarity (secondary, 2026)
Proposed 21+ rules (not law)SB 478 (2025) proposed 21+ for craft hemp flower/THC products — died in conference Apr 2025. SB 250 (2026) proposed 21+ and ATC permits — not enacted. 2026 HB 1285 (introduced) proposed defining low-THC hemp extract with a 0.4 mg/container cap and prohibiting sale/purchase/possession/transport of THC products by minors — final status Verify; it is not presented here as enacted.SB 478 / SB 250 (Run 1); HB 1285 summary via BillTrack50 (secondary)

12.3 Impaired driving — Indiana IS a per se metabolite state, with a narrow defence

IC 9-30-5-1 (verbatim from LawServer mirror — Verify against iga.in.gov):

“(c) A person who operates a vehicle with a controlled substance listed in schedule I or II of IC 35-48-2 or its metabolite in the person’s blood commits a Class C misdemeanor.” “(d) It is a defense to subsection (c) that: (1) the accused person consumed the controlled substance in accordance with a valid prescription or order of a practitioner … who acted in the course of the practitioner’s professional practice; or (2) the: (A) controlled substance is marijuana or a metabolite of marijuana; (B) person was not intoxicated; (C) person did not cause a traffic accident; and (D) substance was identified by means of a chemical test taken pursuant to IC 9-30-7.” LawServer IC 9-30-5-1 — last amended P.L.49-2021, §1 (defence added eff. 1 June 2021, per secondary analysis, Banks & Brower, 22 June 2021).

  • Yes, there is a per se THC-metabolite rule. No minimum concentration, no impairment showing, and no distinction between active THC and inactive metabolite is required for the §9-30-5-1(c) charge. Metabolites can persist days to weeks, so lawful out-of-state use can still produce an Indiana charge — the defence in (d)(2) is the only escape, and it fails if the person was intoxicated, caused an accident, or was not tested under IC 9-30-7.
  • Death / catastrophic injury: IC 9-30-5-5(a)(2) — a person who causes death or catastrophic injury when operating a vehicle “with a controlled substance listed in schedule I or II of IC 35-48-2 or its metabolite in the person’s blood” commits a Level 4 felony. In Wemes v. State, 25A-CR-2491 (Ind. Ct. App. 3 June 2026), the court held §9-30-5-5(a)(2) “provides a flat ban on driving with any amount of [a Schedule I substance or its metabolite]” and “does not require a showing of impairment or intoxication,” and that hemp’s legal status / THC concentration is irrelevant to that subsection. Wemes opinion, public.courts.in.gov.
  • Intoxication-based OWI also exists (IC 9-30-5-1(a)–(b) alcohol thresholds; “while intoxicated” generally) — the metabolite charge is additional, not the only route.
  • Schedule III split effect in Indiana: Because Indiana has no state-licensed medical programme, there is no Indiana medical-marijuana user to whom a Schedule III prescription defence could attach. The (d)(1) prescription defence would on its face cover an FDA-approved prescription (e.g. Epidiolex, Marinol/Syndros, Cesamet) consumed per a valid prescription — Verify that application, as no Indiana case was located applying (d)(1) to a cannabinoid prescription. Adult-use marijuana remains Schedule I in Indiana, so the metabolite rule is unaffected for adult-use users.
  • Implied consent / refusal: IC 9-30-7-5 — refusal penalties and licence suspension (secondary summary: Class C infraction + 1-year suspension, 2 years with a prior OWI conviction — Verify current §9-30-7-5 text).
  • No Indiana per se blood-THC concentration limit (e.g. 5 ng/mL) was located. Indiana’s rule is presence-based, not concentration-based.

12.4 Expungement / record clearing — IC 35-38-9, the “Second Chance Law”

No automatic marijuana expungement programme exists. Indiana expungement is petition-based except for the dismissal/acquittal track below, and it is not marijuana-specific — marijuana offences are expunged, if at all, under the general sections.

TrackWaiting period / routeFeeSource / status
Arrest with no conviction — petition, IC 35-38-9-1Petition 1 year after arrest (or earlier with prosecutor consent).No filing fee for non-conviction petitions.Secondary: Brinkley Law (no fee for §9-1 petitions); court-services guide updated 1 July 2026 (cited in secondary) — Verify fee against the current Trial Court Fee Manual
Dismissal / acquittal — court-orderedCourt orders records expunged; secondary sources state the order takes effect no sooner than 60 days after entry. Verify the 60-day effective-date rule in current §9-1 text.No fee (as above).Secondary county guides — Verify
Misdemeanour conviction, IC 35-38-9-25 years after conviction. Plus a lookback: no other convictions in the preceding 5 years (secondary summary of the statute’s lookback — Verify).Civil filing fee, reported as $157 per county (secondary: Clark County Clerk fee schedule; LegalClarity cost guide). Verify against the current fee manual — the $157 figure is a bundle ($100 base + $20 judicial salaries + $20 automated record-keeping + surcharges, per secondary). Fee may be reduced/waived for indigency (IC 33-37-3-2).LegalClarity (secondary)
Class D / Level 6 felony (no bodily injury), IC 35-38-9-38 years after conviction.$157 per county (as above).As above
Other felonies without serious bodily injury, IC 35-38-9-4Later of 8 years after conviction or 3 years after sentence ends. Prosecutor consent may be required for the more serious categories (see §9-5).$157 per county.Secondary summary — Verify
Remaining eligible felonies, IC 35-38-9-5Later of 10 years after conviction or 5 years after sentence ends. Prosecutor’s written consent required for §9-5 petitions (secondary — Verify).$157 per county.Secondary summary — Verify
Effect — §§9-2/9-3 vs. §§9-4/9-5Records expunged under §§9-4 and 9-5 stay public but are marked expunged (IC 35-38-9-7(b), per secondary). Records under §§9-2/9-3 are sealed from public access; law enforcement retains access. Do not promise physical destruction or universal erasure.—Secondary — Verify §9-7(b)
One-petition limit“A person may file one expungement petition in a lifetime, with petitions filed within one 365-day period counted together” (secondary summary of IC 35-38-9-9 / §9-10 practice). County petitions must be filed together per county; separate counties require separate petitions (and separate fees). Verify the exact lifetime/365-day rule in current statute text — this is the highest-risk expungement detail to get wrong.—Secondary — Verify
Other conditions (secondary, to verify)No charges pending; sentence completed including supervised release; all fines, court costs, and restitution satisfied; driver’s licence not currently suspended / no pending suspension; sex-or-violent-offender status and felony-causing-death convictions excluded.—Secondary summaries — Verify each condition in §§9-2–9-9

Marijuana-specific application: A misdemeanour possession conviction (IC 35-48-4-11, Class B/A) is expungeable under §9-2 after 5 years if all conditions are met. A Level 6 dealing/possession felony is expungeable under §9-3 after 8 years (if no bodily injury and otherwise eligible). Higher-level dealing felonies fall under §§9-4/9-5 with the longer waits and consent requirements. Conditional discharge (IC 35-48-4-12) is not expungement: if the court defers entry of conviction and the person completes conditions, no conviction is entered — so there is no conviction to expunge. If conditions are violated, the conviction is entered and the §9-2 clock starts from that conviction. Effect on hemp licensing: Unavailable / Verify (see §11.8). No source located states that an expunged conviction is disregarded for IC 15-15-13-7’s 10-year bar, nor that it still counts. Effect on gun rights: Expungement/sealing can restore “proper person” status for a handgun licence in some cases (see §12.6) — but that is a handgun-licence consequence, not a federal §922(g) consequence, and a felony drug conviction’s federal firearms disability is a separate question. Verify with counsel for any individual case.

12.5 Landlord / tenant

  • No marijuana-specific Indiana landlord/tenant statute was located. There is no Indiana statute that says “a landlord may / may not evict for marijuana use,” “a tenant may use medical marijuana in a rental,” or “CBD use is protected in housing.” The governing framework is general: lease terms (most Indiana leases prohibit illegal activity, drug possession/manufacture/delivery, and smoking — the sample lease language located prohibits tenant/guest/occupant arrest/conviction for controlled-substance/marijuana/paraphernalia offences and the presence of illegal drugs/paraphernalia on the premises); IC 32-31-6-6 (cited in secondary sources) allows termination where a tenant engages in illegal activities such as drug offences on the rental property; IC 32-31-1-8 and IC 32-30-8-5 are cited for illegal-use-of-premises eviction in secondary guides.
  • Notice-period conflict — Verify, do not assert: one secondary guide says “standard 45-day illegal drug activity procedures apply” for marijuana, while another says illegal activity warrants a 5-day notice to vacate without opportunity to remedy, and a third gives 10 days for lease violations generally. These cannot all be the current rule for the same case; the notice period depends on the lease, the specific statutory ground invoked, and whether the tenancy is subsidised. Do not publish a single notice number for marijuana evictions.
  • CBD / low-THC hemp extract in housing: Lawful possession of CBD/hemp extract is not itself illegal activity, so it is not an illegal-activity ground for eviction. A landlord can enforce a no-smoking lease clause against smoking any substance, including hemp/CBD, and can enforce a no-smoking building rule. Secondary guides agree: “The landlord can't evict you just for having CBD products unless the lease prohibits smoking, period.” Rental Awareness; RentSafe Lease (secondary), accessed 28–29 Oct 2026.
  • Public / subsidised housing — federal, not Indiana, rule: HUD bars admission of households with a member illegally using a controlled substance, and public housing authorities may (and under HUD guidance, must have policies allowing them to) terminate assistance for drug-related activity. Marijuana use is not a reasonable accommodation for disability in public housing (HUD guidance, cited in secondary sources). HUD’s smoke-free rule (eff. 31 July 2018/2019 in the sources) prohibits tobacco smoking in all indoor public-housing areas and within 25 feet of buildings. It does not itself ban marijuana — marijuana is already banned in HUD housing by the controlled-substance prohibition, which is independent of the smoke-free rule. The smoke-free rule does not extend to Section 8 voucher properties (privately owned), where the landlord sets smoking policy but the controlled-substance ban still rides with the voucher. A tenant evicted from federally assisted housing for drug-related activity is ineligible for readmission for 3 years unless they complete a rehabilitation programme (42 U.S.C. §13661, cited in secondary sources — Verify current HUD/PIH guidance for Indiana PHAs). No Indiana-specific PHA marijuana policy was located (e.g. Indianapolis, Fort Wayne, Evansville housing authorities) — each PHA’s Admissions and Continued Occupancy Policy must be checked individually. Unavailable as a compiled Indiana list. FDA-approved cannabinoid drugs (Epidiolex, Marinol, Syndros, Cesamet) are not medical marijuana and are not barred by the HUD marijuana prohibition (secondary, citing HUD/PHLC guidance) — Verify against current HUD guidance before advising a tenant.

12.6 Gun ownership conflicts

  • Federal — 18 U.S.C. §922(g)(3): It is unlawful for a person “who is an unlawful user of or addicted to any controlled substance” to possess firearms or ammunition. ATF’s 2011 open letter to federal firearms licensees states a person who uses marijuana is prohibited “regardless of whether his or her State has passed legislation authorizing marijuana use for medicinal purposes,” and a dealer with reasonable cause to believe a buyer holds a state medical card may not transfer a firearm to that person. ATF Form 4473 Question 21.f asks whether the buyer is an unlawful user of / addicted to marijuana or any other controlled substance; answering falsely is a separate federal felony.
  • Indiana — no medical-card wrinkle, but the conflict is still live: Because Indiana has no state medical programme, there is no Indiana medical-card holder to whom the ATF card-holder guidance could attach, and no Indiana statute creates a marijuana-user gun exception. The conflict in Indiana is the ordinary one: any unlawful user of marijuana (Schedule I in Indiana) is federally prohibited, and any Indiana drug conviction can independently disqualify the person under Indiana law (below).
  • Schedule III split effect in Indiana: The federal baseline moves state-licensed medical marijuana and FDA-approved products to Schedule III eff. 28 Apr 2026 (91 FR 22714). That does not create a lawful Indiana medical user, and §922(g)(3) on its face applies to unlawful users of any controlled substance, not only Schedule I substances — so Schedule III status alone does not remove the federal disability. The Supreme Court’s narrow as-applied holding in United States v. Hemani, No. 24-1234 (18 June 2026, Gorsuch) — a status-only §922(g)(3) prosecution of a regular marijuana user for in-home possession with no danger showing is inconsistent with the Second Amendment — is a federal development whose application to Indiana prosecutions (Seventh Circuit) was not located in this run. Verify before advising any Indiana gun owner; do not present Hemani as an Indiana rule or as a general permission to possess firearms while using marijuana. ATF’s interim final rule eff. 22 Jan 2026 (regular use continuing into the present, 27 CFR 478.11) is likewise federal and was not located as an Indiana-adopted rule.
  • Indiana carry law: Indiana is a permitless-carry (“constitutional carry”) state since 2022 (HEA 1296): a person who may lawfully possess a handgun may carry it without a licence, but a licence to carry is still available and still requires “proper person” status. IC 35-47-1-7, “Proper person,” verbatim in substance: a proper person “(1) does not have a conviction for resisting law enforcement … within five (5) years …; (2) does not have a conviction for a crime for which the person could have been sentenced for more than one (1) year; (3) does not have a conviction for a crime of domestic violence …, unless a court has restored the person’s right to possess a firearm under IC 35-47-4-7; (4) is not prohibited by a court order from possessing a handgun; (5) does not have a record of being an alcohol or drug abuser as defined in this chapter; (6) does not have documented evidence which would give rise to a reasonable belief that the person has a propensity for violent or emotionally unstable conduct; (7) does not make a false statement of material fact …; (8) does not have a conviction for any crime involving an inability to safely handle a handgun; (9) does not have a conviction for violation of [IC 35-47] within five (5) years …; (10) does not have a [felony-equivalent] delinquent-child adjudication [if under 23]; (11) has not been involuntarily committed …; (12) has not been the subject of a 90-day or regular commitment under IC 12-26-6 / -7; (13) has not been found mentally incompetent…” Justia IC 35-47-1-7. “Drug abuser” in the chapter is defined by reference to two or more violations (dealing in cocaine/narcotics/Schedule I–V substances), one of which resulted in conviction or treatment within five years before application (county sheriff guidance summarising the chapter — Verify the definition section, IC 35-47-1-4 / -5, for the exact text). IC 35-47-4-7 provides a court-restoration route for domestic-violence firearm disabilities; no equivalent Indiana restoration route for drug-user disabilities was located — expungement is the practical route discussed in secondary sources, and it is Verify for any individual.
  • Hemp (lawful) vs. marijuana (unlawful) for guns: Lawful hemp/CBD use is not unlawful use of a controlled substance and does not itself trigger §922(g)(3). But a hemp product that is in fact over-limit or, under the AG’s reading, a Schedule I THC variant, and that the user consumes, could be treated as unlawful use — and a workplace/drug-test positive for marijuana metabolites does not by itself prove §922(g)(3) status. No Indiana guidance was located on hemp-CBD use and firearms; this is an inference from the federal definition, flagged as such.

12.7 Pet / veterinary products

  • Indiana-specific — OISC pet-food/treat handout (strongest Indiana source in this Part): “CBD and other hemp derived products are prohibited for use in animal food/treats in Indiana.” Animal foods/treats also “cannot indicate that the product is intended for diagnosis, cure, mitigation, treatment, or prevention of disease.” OISC Indiana Pet Treat Sales Information Handout.
  • Indiana Board of Veterinary Medicine: No Indiana Board CBD/hemp advisory, position statement, or rule was located in this run. The Board’s membership/meeting page was located (in.gov vetboard meetings) but no CBD guidance appears on it. Do not import another state’s veterinary board statement (a Louisiana board newsletter on CBD was returned in search and is expressly not used here — it is not Indiana authority).
  • Federal (FDA) position, which Indiana veterinarians operate under: FDA has approved no animal drug containing cannabis or a cannabis-derived compound such as CBD. FDA-approved human products are Epidiolex (purified CBD), Marinol/Syndros (dronabinol, synthetic THC), and Cesamet (nabilone) — none is an animal drug. A product marketed for animals with therapeutic claims that has not been FDA-approved is an unapproved animal drug and is considered “unsafe” under the FD&C Act. CBD in animal food/treats is treated by FDA as an unapproved food additive / adulterated food; FDA’s Jan 2025 Federal Register notice states no FDA-approved, conditionally approved, or indexed animal drugs contain CBD and that significant data gaps exist on CBD in animals. AVMA position (secondary, via GVMA/AVMA FAQ): veterinarians should exercise caution; FDA and AVMA caution pet owners against use of unapproved cannabis products in animals.
  • Practical Indiana position: A veterinarian in Indiana may discuss hemp/CBD with a client, but may not prescribe, dispense, or recommend it as an approved animal drug; selling CBD pet treats in Indiana runs into the OISC prohibition above as well as the FDA position. Whether mere discussion (as opposed to prescribing/recommending) is disciplined by the Indiana Board is Verify — no Indiana disciplinary case was located.

12.8 CBD / low-THC hemp extract use in schools

  • No Indiana Department of Education statewide CBD/hemp-extract guidance was located. There is no IDOE rule, memo, or model policy on CBD in schools found in this run. School policy is therefore district-by-district, under the general medication-administration statutes.
  • General Indiana school-medication framework: IC 20-33-8-13 (self-administered medication): a student may possess and self-administer medication only with (1) a parent/guardian authorisation filed with the principal and (2) a physician’s written statement that the student has an acute/chronic disease or medical condition for which the physician prescribed medication, that the student was instructed in self-administration, and that the condition requires emergency administration. The authorisation and statement must be filed annually. This framework is built for prescribed medication — it does not on its face authorise self-administration of a non-prescription, non-FDA-approved CBD product. Verify how any district applies §8-13 to CBD. School medication policies generally require medication to be FDA-approved, in original/pharmacy-labelled containers, delivered by a parent/guardian, and administered only by a nurse or principal-designated employee, with documentation.
  • District examples located (illustrative only — not statewide law): Shelbyville Central Schools (C525, Shelby County) policy: “Prior to administering a low hemp THC extract substance, including CBD oil, all criteria listed in IC 24-4-21 and SEA 52 must be met.” The same policy states the corporation does not honour requests to administer over-the-counter herbs/minerals/vitamins/homeopathic products for lack of standardisation/FDA approval. Shelbyville Central Schools C525 policy PDF. Other districts’ handbooks located in search state that “Medication containing Cannabidiol (CBD) oil or THC-A oil will not be administered by school personnel” and that non-FDA-approved treatments (such as CBD oil) may only be administered by a parent/guardian or designated adult (not the school nurse), with the medication not stored on school property. Those are district policies, not IDOE guidance, and the specific Indiana districts were not confirmed in this run — Verify before citing any district by name.
  • What is settled for Indiana schools: Marijuana possession/use on school property is a criminal offence (IC 35-48-4-11) and a school-discipline ground; there is no “medical marijuana at school” right in Indiana because there is no medical programme. Smokable hemp is banned (IC 35-48-4-10.1); a student possessing smokable hemp on school property faces the same criminal/discipline exposure as marijuana possession, subject to the conditional-discharge provision for first misdemeanour possession. Tobacco/vaping on school property is separately prohibited under tobacco law and school policy; a hemp-extract vape device on school property would be treated under the school’s tobacco/vaping policy at minimum — Verify the current school-tobacco statute for hemp devices. No Indiana rule located requiring a school nurse to administer CBD, permitting a student to self-carry CBD, or addressing CBD in an IEP/504 plan. Any such accommodation would be district- and case-specific. Unavailable as a statewide rule.

Employment note (cross-reference to Run 1 Part 1 §9, confirmed in Run 2): No Indiana employment-protection statute for cannabis/hemp use was located, and no hemp-specific staff drug-test rule exists (Part 11 §8). General employment law applies; an employer may test and act on a positive marijuana-metabolite result under its own policy, subject to general Indiana employment law — and, for safety-sensitive / DOT roles, federal testing rules. Do not present any “employee protection” for lawful out-of-state use or for CBD use as Indiana law.

RESEARCH NOTES — BOTH RUNS (gaps, unverified items, conflicting figures, follow-ups)

Read this section before relying on any single Part. A fact marked Verify in Parts 0–6 or 7–12 is not settled law; an item marked Unavailable means no source was located in that run, not that the thing does not exist. Where Run 2 resolved a Run 1 flag, that is stated; where it did not, the flag is carried forward unchanged.

A. Run 1 flags RESOLVED or refined in Run 2

Run 1 flagRun 2 disposition
SB 250 direct iga confirmation (text-fetch failure in Run 1)Resolved for Senate passage and House history: iga actions page, checked 28 Oct 2026 — Senate Roll Call 123, yeas 35, nays 13, 28 Jan 2026; House committee report “amend do pass, adopted” 19 Feb 2026; last listed action 23 Feb 2026 (cosponsors added); no House passage or enactment listed. The iga page carries no “died” label; “not enacted in the 2026 session” is inferred from absence of passage plus session end, plus secondary reports (not called for House second reading before 24 Feb 2026 deadline; SB 144 conference language removed 27 Feb 2026).
SB 250 Senate vote 35–13 vs 35–15 conflictResolved in favour of 35–13 on the iga actions page. The 35–15 secondary figure is superseded and should not be used.
SB 478 “passed the Senate only” shorthandCorrected: SB 478 (2025) passed Senate 40–9 (20 Feb 2025, Roll Call 202) and House 60–37 (15 Apr 2025, Roll Call 455), then died in conference (Senate dissented 16 Apr 2025; conferees/advisors 16–17 Apr 2025; no conference agreement). It is the closest any Indiana hemp-regulation bill has come to enactment.
Paraphernalia dealing shorthand (Class A misdemeanour)Corrected: IC 35-48-4-8.5(a) is a Class A infraction; (b) knowing/intentional violation is a Class A misdemeanour, Level 6 felony on a prior unrelated judgment/conviction under the section. See Part 12 §1.
Run 1 fee picture ($750 / $1,500)Refined, not contradicted: $750 / $1,500 are the amounts actually collected (LSA). The USDA Plan cap is $1,000 per licence / $2,000 for both. The 2020 press figure of $1,000 matches the cap, not the current collected amount. Do not quote $1,000 as the current fee.
HB 1390 advertising ban — absent from Run 1Added in Run 2 (Part 9 §2): HB 1390 (2025) enacted (Public Law 227-2025), effective upon passage (new IC 24-4-26), prohibits advertising “by any medium” of a product containing marijuana or a Schedule I controlled substance. Code section (IC 24-4-26), effective date (upon passage) and penalty schedule (IC 24-4-26-5) confirmed from the enrolled act; whether any enforcement action has been filed remains Verify.
Seed-distributor fee ($45/brand, pre-2026 mirrors)Flagged for recheck: HB 1192 (2026), P.L.82, effective 1 July 2026, amended IC 15-15-1-34 and added §34.5 with per-100-pounds inspection fees ($0.30 / $0.15). Do not present $45/brand as definitively current after 1 July 2026 without re-reading current IC 15-15-1-34 / -34.5.

Run 1’s file is not superseded by this file except for the four corrections listed at the top of this file (paraphernalia dealing, SB 478 passage, HB 1390 addition, HB 1192 seed-law change) and the SB 250 iga confirmation. For any filing, licence application, or enforcement decision, recheck the current official Indiana Code on iga.in.gov, particularly for the 2025 recodification of IC 35-48-1 to IC 35-48-1.1 and for any 2027-session change.
